# Item

## id
CON-FND-4C5290971D40D1

## label
The ventral portion of the fourth pharyngeal arch cartilage gives rise to the thyroid cartilage

## canonical_key
embryology.pharyngeal-arch.fourth-arch-thyroid-cartilage

## definition
The laryngeal cartilages develop from the cartilage bars of the fourth and sixth pharyngeal arches, not from the third or fifth. The ventral (paired) portions of the fourth arch cartilage fuse in the midline to form the thyroid cartilage, while the sixth arch cartilage forms the cricoid, arytenoid and corniculate cartilages. There is no fifth pharyngeal arch cartilage bar in humans — the fifth arch is transient and leaves no persistent skeletal derivative — and the stapes and styloid process derive from the second (hyoid) arch cartilage, not the third.

## explicit_objective
State that the fourth pharyngeal arch cartilage's ventral portion forms the thyroid cartilage, and that the fifth arch has no persisting cartilage derivative.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
fnd

## primary_node_id
DIS-EMB-T03

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Pharyngeal arch cartilage derivatives

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Fourth pharyngeal arch|Laryngeal cartilage development|Thyroid cartilage origin

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing which arch's cartilage forms which laryngeal cartilage — thyroid cartilage is fourth-arch, cricoid/arytenoid/corniculate are sixth-arch, and no cartilage derivative exists for a 'fifth arch' since it does not persist.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-179C6C75DFDC76

## label
The second pharyngeal arch gives rise to the stylohyoid muscle

## canonical_key
embryology.pharyngeal-arch.second-arch-stylohyoid-muscle

## definition
The second (hyoid) pharyngeal arch is innervated by the facial nerve (CN VII) and gives rise to the muscles of facial expression, stapedius, stylohyoid, and the posterior belly of digastric. The anterior belly of digastric and mylohyoid are first-arch (trigeminal, V3) derivatives, stylopharyngeus is a third-arch (glossopharyngeal, IX) derivative, and geniohyoid is not a pharyngeal-arch muscle at all — it is a hypoglossal-innervated derivative of the occipital myotomes.

## explicit_objective
Identify the stylohyoid muscle as a second pharyngeal arch (facial nerve) derivative, distinct from first-, third-arch and non-arch muscles of the neck.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
fnd

## primary_node_id
DIS-EMB-T03

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Head and neck embryology

## subtopic
Pharyngeal arch muscular derivatives

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Second pharyngeal arch|Hyoid arch muscles|Stylohyoid muscle origin

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning stylohyoid to the first arch by analogy with the nearby digastric — only its posterior belly is second-arch; the anterior belly is first-arch. Geniohyoid is often mistaken for an arch muscle because of its name, but it is a hypoglossal-innervated non-arch muscle.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-4309219C3BC6EE

## label
The connective tissue layer is the vascular layer of the scalp

## canonical_key
anatomy.scalp.connective-tissue-vascular-layer

## definition
The scalp has five layers, classically remembered by the mnemonic SCALP: Skin, dense subcutaneous Connective tissue, epicranial Aponeurosis (galea aponeurotica), Loose areolar tissue, and Pericranium. The second layer — the dense subcutaneous connective tissue — is the vascular and nerve-bearing layer: the scalp's arteries and cutaneous nerves run within it, tethered to the skin above and the aponeurosis below by fibrous septa, which is also why scalp wounds in this layer bleed profusely and why vessels do not retract.

## explicit_objective
Identify the dense subcutaneous connective tissue layer as the vascular layer of the scalp, within the five-layer SCALP scheme.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
fnd

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Head and neck anatomy

## subtopic
Scalp layers

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
SCALP mnemonic|Scalp vascular layer|Subcutaneous layer of scalp

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming the aponeurotic layer (galea) is the vascular layer because it is the strongest, fibrous layer — it is tendinous and relatively avascular; the vessels and nerves run in the connective tissue layer immediately superficial to it.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-1D667C44DC5A49

## label
The greater petrosal nerve carries preganglionic parasympathetic fibers to the lacrimal gland

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.cranial-nerve.greater-petrosal-lacrimal-pathway

## definition
Preganglionic parasympathetic fibers to the lacrimal gland originate in the superior salivatory nucleus of the facial nerve (CN VII) and leave the facial nerve as the greater petrosal nerve, which joins the deep petrosal (sympathetic) nerve to form the nerve of the pterygoid canal; this reaches the pterygopalatine ganglion, where the parasympathetic fibers synapse, and postganglionic fibers travel via the zygomatic and lacrimal nerves to the lacrimal gland. The chorda tympani, by contrast, is the facial nerve branch supplying the submandibular and sublingual glands, not the lacrimal gland, and the zygomaticotemporal and lesser petrosal nerves belong to different pathways (the lesser petrosal carries parotid parasympathetics from the glossopharyngeal nerve).

## explicit_objective
Trace the preganglionic parasympathetic supply to the lacrimal gland through the greater petrosal nerve, the pterygopalatine ganglion, and its postganglionic route.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Cranial nerves

## subtopic
Facial nerve parasympathetic pathways

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Greater petrosal nerve|Lacrimal gland innervation|Pterygopalatine ganglion pathway

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the greater petrosal (lacrimal gland) pathway with the chorda tympani (submandibular/sublingual gland) pathway — both are facial-nerve parasympathetic outflows but reach different glands via different ganglia (pterygopalatine vs submandibular).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-9982FA6261208E

## label
The body of the caudate nucleus is a suprathalamic structure

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.basal-ganglia.caudate-body-suprathalamic

## definition
The caudate nucleus is a C-shaped structure with a head, body and tail that arches over the thalamus. Its body runs along the floor of the body of the lateral ventricle, directly superior to the thalamus, which is why the body of the caudate is classed as a suprathalamic structure, separated from the thalamus by the stria terminalis and thalamostriate vein. The head of the caudate lies anterior and lateral to the anterior horn of the lateral ventricle (not directly above the thalamus), the tail curves inferiorly into the temporal horn alongside the amygdala, and the lentiform nucleus (putamen + globus pallidus) sits lateral to the thalamus rather than above it.

## explicit_objective
Identify the body of the caudate nucleus as the suprathalamic part of the caudate, distinguishing it from the head, tail, and lentiform nucleus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Caudate nucleus body|Suprathalamic structures|Basal ganglia topography

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming the whole caudate nucleus is suprathalamic — only the body runs directly over the thalamus; the head is anterior to it and the tail curves down into the temporal lobe.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A897156CACCE08

## label
The vagus nerve runs in the carotid sheath between the internal jugular vein and the common carotid artery

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.cranial-nerve.vagus-carotid-sheath-course

## definition
Within the carotid sheath in the neck, the vagus nerve (CN X) descends in the posterior groove between the internal jugular vein (laterally) and the internal/common carotid artery (medially), giving off the pharyngeal and superior/recurrent laryngeal branches along its course before entering the thorax. The hypoglossal nerve crosses superficial to both the internal and external carotid arteries but does not run within the carotid sheath itself; the glossopharyngeal nerve passes between the internal and external carotid arteries more superiorly, and the accessory nerve exits the sheath early to cross the posterior triangle.

## explicit_objective
Locate the vagus nerve within the carotid sheath, running in the groove between the internal jugular vein and the carotid artery.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Vagus nerve course in the neck

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Vagus nerve carotid sheath|Vagus nerve course|Carotid sheath contents

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the vagus nerve's position within the carotid sheath with the hypoglossal nerve, which crosses external to the sheath rather than running inside it.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-85DFA113A48E73

## label
The ansa cervicalis supplies the omohyoid muscle

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.cervical-plexus.ansa-cervicalis-omohyoid

## definition
The ansa cervicalis is a loop of the cervical plexus (C1-C3) that supplies most of the infrahyoid ('strap') muscles: sternohyoid, sternothyroid and omohyoid, via its superior root (C1 fibers carried on the hypoglossal nerve) and inferior root (C2-C3). Thyrohyoid and geniohyoid, despite sitting among the infrahyoid/suprahyoid muscles, are supplied directly by C1 fibers travelling on the hypoglossal nerve rather than by the ansa cervicalis proper, while mylohyoid (nerve to mylohyoid, V3) and stylohyoid (facial nerve) belong to entirely different nerves.

## explicit_objective
Identify omohyoid as an ansa cervicalis-innervated infrahyoid muscle, distinct from thyrohyoid (direct C1/hypoglossal) and the suprahyoid muscles supplied by other nerves.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Ansa cervicalis distribution

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Ansa cervicalis|Infrahyoid muscle innervation|Omohyoid nerve supply

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming thyrohyoid is ansa cervicalis-innervated because it is an infrahyoid muscle like omohyoid — thyrohyoid instead receives direct C1 fibers riding on the hypoglossal nerve, bypassing the ansa loop.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A6429026075B53

## label
The tectum is the dorsal part of the midbrain

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.brainstem.tectum-midbrain-location

## definition
The tectum (Latin for 'roof') is the dorsal (posterior) part of the midbrain, formed by the corpora quadrigemina — the paired superior colliculi (visual reflexes, saccadic eye movements) and inferior colliculi (auditory relay). The midbrain is conventionally divided into the tectum dorsally, the tegmentum centrally (containing the red nucleus, substantia nigra and cranial nerve nuclei III/IV), and the crus cerebri (cerebral peduncles) ventrally; the tectum is not a subdivision of the pons, diencephalon, or medulla oblongata.

## explicit_objective
Locate the tectum as the dorsal midbrain, formed by the superior and inferior colliculi.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Tectum|Corpora quadrigemina|Midbrain roof

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing the tectum in the diencephalon because the colliculi sit near the thalamus — the tectum is midbrain tissue, immediately caudal to the diencephalon, not part of it.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-088E2369B06007

## label
The great auricular nerve supplies the skin over the angle of the mandible

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.cervical-plexus.great-auricular-nerve-angle-of-mandible

## definition
The great auricular nerve (C2-C3), a cutaneous branch of the cervical plexus, ascends over sternocleidomastoid to supply the skin over the parotid gland, the angle of the mandible, and parts of the auricle. The lesser and greater occipital nerves instead supply the posterior scalp, and the buccal branch of the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve supplies the skin and mucosa of the cheek anteriorly — none of these reach the angle of the mandible, which is cervical-plexus (not trigeminal) territory.

## explicit_objective
Identify the great auricular nerve as the cutaneous supply to the skin over the angle of the mandible, distinguishing it from occipital and trigeminal cutaneous nerves.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Cervical plexus cutaneous branches

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Great auricular nerve|Cervical plexus cutaneous supply|Angle of mandible sensation

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming trigeminal (buccal) territory extends to the angle of the mandible because the trigeminal nerve supplies most of the face — the angle of the mandible and overlying skin instead falls to the cervical plexus's great auricular nerve.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-633754C3665387

## label
The ventral posteromedial (VPM) thalamic nucleus relays facial sensation to the cortical face area

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.thalamus.vpm-facial-sensation-relay

## definition
The ventral posterior nuclear complex of the thalamus is somatotopically divided: the ventral posterolateral (VPL) nucleus relays touch, proprioceptive and pain/temperature information from the body and limbs (via the medial lemniscus and spinothalamic tract), while the ventral posteromedial (VPM) nucleus relays the equivalent sensation, plus taste, from the face and oral cavity (via the trigeminal and gustatory pathways) to the face representation of the primary somatosensory cortex, which corresponds to the lower postcentral gyrus/opercular region often labelled area 43. The medial thalamic group and ventrolateral group serve different relay roles (limbic/associative and motor, respectively), not facial sensation.

## explicit_objective
Identify the VPM as the thalamic relay for facial sensation to the cortical face representation, distinguishing it from the body-relaying VPL.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## microtopic


## nanotopic

## aliases
VPM thalamic nucleus|Facial sensory relay|Ventral posterior thalamus

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Swapping VPL and VPM — VPL relays body/limb sensation, VPM relays face and oral sensation (plus taste) to the corresponding somatosensory cortex representation.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-807317C62E3B36

## label
The vermis and intermediate zone of the cerebellum carry a topographical body map

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.cerebellum.vermis-intermediate-zone-somatotopy

## definition
The cerebellar cortex is functionally zoned in parasagittal strips: the vermis and the flanking intermediate (paravermal) zones together form the spinocerebellum, which receives somatotopically organised proprioceptive input from the spinal cord and carries a topographical representation of the body (trunk medially near the vermis, limbs more laterally in the intermediate zone). The lateral hemispheres (neocerebellum/cerebrocerebellum) instead process cortically-relayed information for planning and coordinating skilled voluntary movement without a comparable direct body map, and the flocculonodular lobe (archicerebellum/vestibulocerebellum) is concerned with balance and eye movements, not somatotopic representation.

## explicit_objective
Identify the vermis and intermediate zone as the cerebellar regions carrying a topographical body map, distinguishing them from the lateral hemispheres and flocculonodular lobe.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Cerebellum

## subtopic
Cerebellar functional zones

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Spinocerebellum somatotopy|Cerebellar vermis|Intermediate zone cerebellum

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Attributing the body map to the lateral hemispheres because they are the largest cerebellar region — the somatotopic map belongs to the vermis/intermediate (spinocerebellar) zones, while the lateral hemispheres handle movement planning without a direct topographic map.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-8AF0A40355A11E

## label
The face is represented most laterally and inferiorly in the primary motor cortex homunculus

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.motor-cortex.face-lateral-inferior-representation

## definition
The primary motor cortex (precentral gyrus) contains an inverted, disproportionate somatotopic map (the motor homunculus): the leg and foot are represented superiorly and extend onto the medial surface of the hemisphere (paracentral lobule), the trunk and arm occupy the mid-convexity, and the face, lips and tongue — which require the finest, most differentiated motor control — are represented most laterally and inferiorly, near the lateral (Sylvian) fissure, with disproportionately large cortical territory devoted to them.

## explicit_objective
Locate the face representation as the most lateral and inferior part of the primary motor cortex homunculus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Motor cortex

## subtopic
Motor homunculus topography

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Motor homunculus|Primary motor cortex|Face representation

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing the hand, rather than the face, as the most lateral/inferior representation — the hand sits mid-convexity with a large area, but the face representation extends furthest laterally and inferiorly toward the Sylvian fissure.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A32E7D804302CD

## label
The phrenic nucleus is not found in thoracic spinal cord segments

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.spinal-cord.phrenic-nucleus-cervical-not-thoracic

## definition
The thoracic spinal cord grey matter contains the nucleus proprius (dorsal horn), Clark's (dorsal) nucleus (T1-L2, giving rise to the dorsal spinocerebellar tract), and the intermediolateral (sympathetic) nucleus of the lateral horn (T1-L2) — all genuinely thoracic structures. The phrenic nucleus, by contrast, which gives rise to the phrenic nerve supplying the diaphragm, is located in the cervical spinal cord (chiefly C3-C5, 'C3, 4, 5 keep the diaphragm alive'), not in any thoracic segment.

## explicit_objective
State that the phrenic nucleus is a cervical (C3-C5), not thoracic, spinal cord structure, unlike the nucleus proprius, Clark's nucleus and the sympathetic nucleus, which are genuinely thoracic.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Phrenic nucleus location|Thoracic cord nuclei|Cervical origin of phrenic nerve

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming the phrenic nucleus is thoracic because the diaphragm is a chest structure — the diaphragm's motor nucleus is cervical (C3-C5); only its innervated muscle sits in the thorax/abdomen.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-24651D218BE5D0

## label
The superior sagittal sinus runs in the superior fixed border of the falx cerebri

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.dural-venous-sinuses.superior-sagittal-sinus-falx

## definition
The superior sagittal sinus is a midline dural venous sinus that runs within the attached (superior) border of the falx cerebri, from the crista galli anteriorly to the internal occipital protuberance posteriorly, where it typically turns to become the right transverse sinus at the confluence of sinuses. It receives venous blood from the superior cerebral veins and drains arachnoid granulations (CSF absorption), distinguishing its groove and course from the inferior sagittal sinus (running in the free inferior border of the falx, draining into the straight sinus) and the straight sinus (running along the junction of falx and tentorium).

## explicit_objective
Locate the superior sagittal sinus within the superior attached border of the falx cerebri, distinguishing its course from the inferior sagittal and straight sinuses.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## primary_node_id

## microtopic


## nanotopic

## aliases
Superior sagittal sinus|Falx cerebri|Dural venous sinus groove

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the groove for the superior sagittal sinus with that of the inferior sagittal sinus or straight sinus — the superior sagittal sinus occupies the convex, attached superior border of the falx, not its free inferior edge.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-2A9B1A2E782F8B

## label
The cuneate nucleus is the second-order neuron of the dorsal column pathway for the upper limb

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.dorsal-column.cuneate-nucleus-upper-limb-second-order

## definition
The dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway carries fine touch, vibration and conscious proprioception. Fibers from the upper limb and upper trunk (including the finger) ascend in the fasciculus cuneatus and synapse on their first second-order neuron in the cuneate nucleus of the lower medulla; fibers from the lower limb and lower trunk instead ascend in the fasciculus gracilis to synapse in the gracile nucleus. Both nuclei give rise to internal arcuate fibers that decussate to form the medial lemniscus, but only the cuneate nucleus is the second-order relay for finger/upper-limb input, not the gracile nucleus, Clark's nucleus (dorsal spinocerebellar, unconscious) or the substantia gelatinosa (pain modulation).

## explicit_objective
Identify the cuneate nucleus as the second-order neuron for conscious proprioceptive/fine touch input from the finger and upper limb, distinct from the gracile nucleus (lower limb).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic
Dorsal column-medial lemniscus pathway

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Cuneate nucleus|Fasciculus cuneatus|Dorsal column second-order neuron

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Swapping cuneate and gracile nuclei — cuneate relays the upper limb/finger (fasciculus cuneatus), gracile relays the lower limb/trunk (fasciculus gracilis); both are medullary, not spinal.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-98F2C869D2B70D

## label
The hypoglossal nerve rootlets emerge in the anterolateral sulcus between the pyramid and the olive

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.brainstem.hypoglossal-nerve-preolivary-sulcus-tumour

## definition
The hypoglossal nerve (CN XII) rootlets emerge from the medulla in the anterolateral (preolivary) sulcus, the groove lying between the pyramid medially and the olive laterally, then exit the skull through the hypoglossal canal to supply the intrinsic and most extrinsic muscles of the tongue. A mass lesion (e.g. a medullary or foramen magnum tumour) compressing this preolivary groove characteristically damages the hypoglossal rootlets, producing ipsilateral tongue weakness and deviation toward the lesion on protrusion — a distinct clinical localisation from lesions of the vagus, glossopharyngeal or sublingual gland, which are not related to this specific groove.

## explicit_objective
Locate the hypoglossal nerve rootlets in the sulcus between the medullary pyramid and olive, and link compression there to hypoglossal (not vagal or glossopharyngeal) deficits.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Hypoglossal nerve|Preolivary sulcus|Medullary cranial nerve rootlets

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the pyramid-olive (preolivary) groove, which carries hypoglossal rootlets, with the groove behind the olive (postolivary sulcus), which carries the glossopharyngeal, vagus and accessory nerve rootlets instead.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C0C75BD01BFD82

## label
Spinothalamic tract fibers from body receptors terminate in the VPL of the thalamus

## canonical_key
neuroanatomy.spinothalamic-tract.vpl-termination

## definition
The anterolateral (spinothalamic) system carries pain, temperature and crude touch from the body: primary afferents synapse in the dorsal horn, second-order neurons decussate near their segment of entry and ascend in the contralateral anterolateral white matter, terminating in the ventral posterolateral (VPL) nucleus of the thalamus, which then relays this information to the postcentral gyrus (somatosensory cortex). This VPL termination applies to body (limb/trunk) receptors specifically; the corresponding pathway for facial pain/temperature (the trigeminothalamic tract) instead terminates in the VPM, not the VPL, and the insular cortex is a higher-order pain processing area, not the thalamic relay itself.

## explicit_objective
State that spinothalamic (anterolateral) fibers carrying sensation from body receptors terminate in the VPL nucleus of the thalamus, as opposed to VPM (face) or insular cortex.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Spinothalamic tract termination

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Spinothalamic tract|VPL thalamic termination|Anterolateral system

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Sending body-pain-pathway fibers to VPM instead of VPL — VPM is reserved for the trigeminothalamic (facial) pain and sensory pathway; body pathways terminate in VPL.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-7453A240F7D729

## label
The ultimobranchial body is the dorsal-wing derivative of the fourth pharyngeal pouch

## canonical_key
embryology.pharyngeal-pouch.fourth-pouch-dorsal-wing-ultimobranchial-body

## definition
The fourth pharyngeal pouch has a dorsal and a ventral wing, each with its own derivative. In this department's teaching, the dorsal wing continues as the ultimobranchial body, which migrates to fuse with the developing thyroid gland and contributes the parafollicular (C) cells that secrete calcitonin, while the ventral wing forms the superior parathyroid gland. This pairs with the third pharyngeal pouch, whose dorsal wing gives the inferior parathyroid gland and whose ventral wing gives the thymus — a commonly confused four-way grid worth memorising as pouch-by-pouch, wing-by-wing rather than as a flat list.

## explicit_objective
State that the ultimobranchial body is the fourth pharyngeal pouch's dorsal-wing derivative, distinct from the pouch's ventral-wing (parathyroid) derivative and from the third pouch's thymus/inferior-parathyroid pair.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
fnd

## primary_node_id
DIS-EMB-T03

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Pharyngeal pouch embryology

## subtopic
Fourth pouch derivatives

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Ultimobranchial body|Fourth pharyngeal pouch|Parafollicular C cells

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing which pouch (third vs fourth) and which wing (dorsal vs ventral) gives which derivative — this department's grid places the ultimobranchial body on the fourth pouch's dorsal wing, the superior parathyroid on its ventral wing, and the third pouch's dorsal/ventral wings give the inferior parathyroid and thymus respectively.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-0F72ABE1CDF46D

## label
The hypoglossal nerve crosses the internal carotid artery and the first part of the lingual artery during its course

## canonical_key
hypoglossalnerve.course.crosses-internal-carotid-and-lingual-artery

## definition
The hypoglossal nerve (CN XII) emerges from the preolivary sulcus of the medulla, exits the skull through the hypoglossal canal, and descends in the neck lateral to the vagus nerve and internal/external carotid arteries before curving forward. On its way to the tongue it crosses superficial to both the internal carotid artery and the first (proximal) part of the lingual artery, a relationship surgeons use when identifying the nerve during neck dissections, before passing deep to the posterior belly of digastric and looping over the loop of the lingual artery to reach the tongue's intrinsic and extrinsic muscles.

## explicit_objective
State that the hypoglossal nerve crosses superficial to the internal carotid artery and the first part of the lingual artery during its cervical course to the tongue.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Hypoglossal nerve cervical course

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Hypoglossal nerve|CN XII|Lingual artery relations

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing the hypoglossal nerve within the carotid sheath alongside the vagus nerve — it instead crosses external to the sheath, superficial to the carotid vessels, on its way to the tongue.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4DF9D89BF9F4FC

## label
Area 22 of the cerebral cortex is located in the superior temporal gyrus

## canonical_key
cerebralcortex.area22.superior-temporal-gyrus-location

## definition
Brodmann area 22 occupies the superior temporal gyrus, largely overlapping Wernicke's area in the dominant (usually left) hemisphere, where it participates in receptive language comprehension. It sits within the primary/association auditory cortical territory of the temporal lobe rather than in the frontal, occipital or parietal lobes, so localising it correctly to the superior temporal gyrus is the key discriminator tested against those other lobar options.

## explicit_objective
Locate Brodmann area 22 in the superior temporal gyrus, overlapping Wernicke's area.

## concept_type
definition

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing area 22 (superior temporal gyrus, receptive language) with area 44/45 (Broca's area, inferior frontal gyrus, expressive language) — the two are on opposite sides of the lateral fissure and serve complementary language functions.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-7FC23ED37C7C8B

## label
The internal capsule is supplied by the anterior choroidal, lenticulostriate (middle cerebral) and recurrent (anterior cerebral) arteries, but not by the posterior cerebral artery

## canonical_key
internalcapsule.arterialsupply.anterior-choroidal-mca-aca-not-pca

## definition
The internal capsule receives its arterial supply from several sources acting on different parts: lenticulostriate branches of the middle cerebral artery supply the genu and much of the posterior limb, the recurrent artery of Heubner (from the anterior cerebral artery) supplies the anterior limb, and the anterior choroidal artery supplies the retrolenticular and posterior parts of the posterior limb. The posterior cerebral artery, by contrast, chiefly supplies the occipital lobe and (via separate thalamoperforating branches) the thalamus, and is not counted among the internal capsule's named supplying vessels.

## explicit_objective
State that the internal capsule is supplied by the anterior choroidal, middle cerebral (lenticulostriate) and anterior cerebral (Heubner) arteries, but not by the posterior cerebral artery.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode

## subject

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Internal capsule arterial supply

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Internal capsule|Anterior choroidal artery|Lenticulostriate arteries

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming the posterior cerebral artery supplies the internal capsule because it supplies the nearby thalamus — the two structures have distinct, non-overlapping named arterial suppliers.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-6FABEEDE721203

## label
Abnormal development of the tuberculum impar causes a bifid tongue

## canonical_key
embryology.tongue.tuberculum-impar-anomaly-bifid-tongue

## definition
The anterior two-thirds of the tongue develops from two lateral lingual swellings and a small midline tuberculum impar, all first-arch derivatives; normally the lateral swellings overgrow and fuse across the midline, burying the tuberculum impar. Failure of the lateral lingual swellings to fuse properly over the tuberculum impar leaves a persistent midline groove, producing a bifid (cleft) tongue, distinct from ankyloglossia (a short lingual frenulum) or ectopic thyroid tissue problems (foramen cecum/thyroglossal duct anomalies), which arise from different developmental structures.

## explicit_objective
State that failure of the lateral lingual swellings to fuse over the tuberculum impar produces a bifid tongue.

definition

## support_mode

## subject

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Tuberculum impar|Bifid tongue|Tongue development

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing tuberculum impar fusion failure (bifid tongue) with ankyloglossia (a short frenulum, unrelated to the tuberculum impar) or with foramen cecum/thyroglossal duct anomalies (a different midline structure, the origin of the thyroid diverticulum).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-7E6D7C2D861193

## label
A lesion of the optic tract causes contralateral homonymous hemianopia

## canonical_key
opticpathway.optictract-lesion.contralateral-homonymous-hemianopia

## definition
The optic tract carries fibers from the temporal (uncrossed) half of the ipsilateral retina and the nasal (crossed) half of the contralateral retina — that is, fibers representing the contralateral visual field from both eyes — from the optic chiasm to the lateral geniculate body. A lesion of the optic tract therefore produces a contralateral homonymous hemianopia (loss of the same half of the visual field in both eyes, opposite the lesion), distinct from a chiasmal lesion (bitemporal hemianopia) or complete unilateral optic nerve transection (monocular blindness).

## explicit_objective
State that an optic tract lesion produces contralateral homonymous hemianopia, distinct from chiasmal (bitemporal) and optic nerve (monocular) visual field defects.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode

## subject

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Optic tract lesion localisation

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Optic tract|Homonymous hemianopia|Visual field defects

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing an optic tract lesion (contralateral homonymous hemianopia) with an optic chiasm lesion (bitemporal hemianopia, from crossing nasal fibers only) or an optic nerve lesion (monocular blindness).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A86CE6A9203C1E

## label
The corpus callosum is a commissural fiber bundle, not a long association fiber tract

## canonical_key
cerebralwhitematter.longassociationfibers.corpus-callosum-excluded-as-commissural

## definition
Cerebral white matter fibers are classed by what they connect: association fibers link cortical areas within the SAME hemisphere (short, arcuate/U-fibers, or long, such as the superior longitudinal bundle, uncinate fasciculus and inferior longitudinal fasciculus), while commissural fibers cross the midline to connect the two hemispheres. The corpus callosum, the largest commissural bundle, therefore does not belong among the long association fibers even though it is a long, well-known white matter tract — its defining feature is inter-hemispheric (commissural) connection, not intra-hemispheric (association) connection.

## explicit_objective
State that the corpus callosum is a commissural fiber bundle, distinct from the long association fibers (superior longitudinal, uncinate and inferior longitudinal fasciculi).

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Cerebral white matter

## subtopic
Association vs commissural fiber classification

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Corpus callosum|Long association fibers|Commissural fibers

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Classing the corpus callosum among the long association fibers because it is a long, prominent tract — length alone does not make a fiber bundle an association tract; the corpus callosum is commissural (inter-hemispheric), not association (intra-hemispheric).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4E02E346391C78

## label
The corticobulbar tract runs through the genu of the internal capsule

## canonical_key
internalcapsule.genu.corticobulbar-tract-fibers

## definition
The internal capsule's genu (the bend between its anterior and posterior limbs) chiefly carries corticobulbar fibers descending to the cranial nerve motor nuclei of the brainstem, distinct from the anterior limb (frontopontine and anterior thalamic radiation fibers) and the posterior limb (corticospinal fibers anteriorly, sensory thalamic radiations posteriorly). This regional organisation is why a small, precisely placed internal capsule lesion at the genu can selectively affect lower-face/tongue/palate motor function without necessarily producing the limb weakness of a posterior-limb corticospinal lesion.

## explicit_objective
Locate the corticobulbar tract within the genu of the internal capsule, distinct from the anterior limb's frontopontine fibers and the posterior limb's corticospinal and sensory radiation fibers.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Internal capsule

## subtopic
Regional fiber content

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Internal capsule genu|Corticobulbar tract|Regional internal capsule anatomy

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming all descending motor fibers in the internal capsule are corticospinal — the corticobulbar (to cranial nerve nuclei) and corticospinal (to spinal cord) tracts occupy different parts, the genu and posterior limb respectively.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-8D5A807ABB9609

## label
The substantia gelatinosa of the dorsal horn is the first major relay/modulation site for pain transmission

## canonical_key
spinalcorddorsalhorn.nucleus.pain-transmission-relay

## definition
The substantia gelatinosa (Rexed lamina II, with lamina I) sits at the tip of the dorsal horn and is the first major site where nociceptive (pain) afferents synapse and are modulated before their signal ascends in the spinothalamic tract, distinct from nucleus proprius (general sensory relay), Clarke's nucleus (unconscious proprioception to the cerebellum) or the cuneate/gracile nuclei (conscious touch/proprioception in the dorsal columns). This modulatory role at the substantia gelatinosa is also the anatomical site targeted by the gate-control mechanism, where large-diameter touch afferents inhibit onward pain transmission.

## explicit_objective
Identify the substantia gelatinosa of the dorsal horn as the spinal grey matter nucleus responsible for pain signal transmission and modulation.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Dorsal horn pain relay nuclei

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Substantia gelatinosa|Dorsal horn|Pain transmission

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the substantia gelatinosa (pain relay/modulation) with Clarke's nucleus (unconscious proprioception, dorsal spinocerebellar tract) or the cuneate/gracile nuclei (conscious touch/proprioception, dorsal columns) — all are grey matter relay points but for different modalities.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F5E118F9279F4A

## label
A lesion of the lateral corticospinal tract causes loss of fine, discrete voluntary movement

## canonical_key
corticospinaltract.lesion.loss-of-fine-voluntary-movement

## definition
The lateral corticospinal tract, the largest and most clinically important descending motor pathway, chiefly controls fine, discrete voluntary movements of the distal limbs (especially the digits), having crossed in the medullary pyramidal decussation before descending in the lateral funiculus of the spinal cord. A lesion of this tract characteristically causes loss of fine, skilled voluntary movement, distinct from the sensory ataxia (dorsal column lesion), imbalance (spinocerebellar/cerebellar lesion) or loss of protective withdrawal reflex (a segmental reflex arc lesion) produced by damage to the other numbered white matter tracts in the same cross-section.

## explicit_objective
State that a lateral corticospinal tract lesion produces loss of fine, discrete voluntary movement, distinguishing it from dorsal column and spinocerebellar tract lesion syndromes.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Spinal cord white matter tracts

## subtopic
Corticospinal tract lesion syndrome

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Corticospinal tract|Lateral corticospinal tract|Fine motor control

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Attributing sensory ataxia to a corticospinal tract lesion — sensory ataxia is a dorsal column (proprioceptive) lesion sign, while the corticospinal tract's own lesion sign is loss of fine voluntary movement (with accompanying upper motor neuron signs).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-21B8B66BE5A8FA

## label
The paracentral lobule carries the motor and sensory representation for the perineum, alongside the leg and foot

## canonical_key
paracentrallobule.representation.leg-foot-and-perineum-sensorimotor-area

## definition
The paracentral lobule is the medial-surface continuation of the pre- and postcentral gyri, and in the sensorimotor homunculus it carries the representation for the leg, foot, and — continuing further onto the medial surface — the perineum, including voluntary control relevant to bladder and bowel sphincters. This is why a paracentral lobule lesion (e.g. from anterior cerebral artery territory infarction or a parasagittal mass) classically produces contralateral leg weakness together with urinary/bowel disturbance, rather than head/neck, trunk or upper limb deficits, which are represented more laterally on the hemispheric convexity.

## explicit_objective
State that the paracentral lobule represents the leg/foot and, further medially, the perineum in the sensorimotor homunculus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Paracentral lobule somatotopy

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Paracentral lobule|Homunculus|Perineal representation

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Limiting the paracentral lobule's territory to the leg/foot alone — it also carries the perineal representation, which is why lesions there can produce bladder/bowel symptoms alongside leg weakness.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-733999202B4B81

## label
Digastric receives its motor supply from two different cranial nerves — the anterior belly from the trigeminal (V3), the posterior belly from the facial (VII)

## canonical_key
digastricmuscle.innervation.dual-cranial-nerve-anterior-v3-posterior-vii

## definition
The digastric muscle has two bellies of different embryological (pharyngeal arch) origin joined by an intermediate tendon: the anterior belly is a first-arch derivative innervated by the mylohyoid nerve, a branch of the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve (V3), while the posterior belly is a second-arch derivative innervated directly by the facial nerve (CN VII). This makes digastric unusual among named muscles for receiving its motor supply from two separate cranial nerves, unlike buccinator (facial nerve alone), trapezius (accessory nerve alone) or omohyoid (ansa cervicalis alone).

## explicit_objective
State that digastric is innervated by two cranial nerves — the mandibular nerve (V3) for its anterior belly, the facial nerve (VII) for its posterior belly.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Digastric dual innervation

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Digastric muscle|Dual cranial nerve innervation|Anterior/posterior belly

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming digastric, like most named muscles, has a single nerve supply — its two bellies are separate pharyngeal arch derivatives with separate cranial nerve supplies, a genuine exception worth remembering by name.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4FB3397E4619F3

## label
The auriculotemporal nerve supplies sensation to the external auditory meatus

## canonical_key
auriculotemporalnerve.sensorysupply.external-auditory-meatus

## definition
The auriculotemporal nerve, a branch of the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve (V3), runs close to the temporomandibular joint and parotid gland to supply cutaneous sensation to the temporal region, the outer surface of the tympanic membrane and, notably, the external auditory meatus — distinct from the nose/nasal septum (other trigeminal branches), the inner (medial) surface of the tympanic membrane (glossopharyngeal nerve territory), and the chin/lower lip (mental nerve territory).

## explicit_objective
State that the auriculotemporal nerve supplies sensation to the external auditory meatus, distinct from the nasal septum, inner eardrum surface, or chin/lower lip.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Auriculotemporal nerve sensory territory

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Auriculotemporal nerve|External auditory meatus|Trigeminal nerve branches

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Attributing sensation of the INNER surface of the tympanic membrane to the auriculotemporal nerve — that territory instead belongs to the glossopharyngeal nerve's tympanic branch (Jacobson's nerve).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-6BDDD009FFC55C

## label
The dorsal spinocerebellar tract arises from the nucleus thoracicus (Clarke's column)

## canonical_key
dorsalspinocerebellartract.origin.nucleus-thoracicus-clarke-column

## definition
The dorsal (posterior) spinocerebellar tract carries unconscious proprioceptive information from the lower limb and trunk to the cerebellum, originating from second-order neurons in the nucleus thoracicus (Clarke's column, or Clarke's nucleus), present at spinal levels T1-L2. This is distinct from the substantia gelatinosa of Rolando (pain modulation), the nucleus proprius (general sensory relay contributing to other ascending tracts) and the cuneate nucleus (conscious proprioception from the upper limb, in the medulla, not the spinal cord).

## explicit_objective
State that the dorsal spinocerebellar tract originates from the nucleus thoracicus (Clarke's column) at spinal levels T1-L2.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Dorsal spinocerebellar tract origin

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Dorsal spinocerebellar tract|Clarke's column|Nucleus thoracicus

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the nucleus of origin for the dorsal spinocerebellar tract (Clarke's column/nucleus thoracicus) with the substantia gelatinosa (pain) or nucleus proprius (general relay) — all are dorsal horn grey matter structures but with different destinations and functions.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-152993798822B6

## label
A lesion of the internal capsule causes spastic paralysis of the contralateral side of the body

## canonical_key
internalcapsule.lesion.contralateral-spastic-hemiplegia

## definition
Because the corticospinal and corticobulbar fibers running through the internal capsule have not yet decussated (they cross later, at the medullary pyramids and at each cranial nerve motor nucleus respectively), a unilateral internal capsule lesion produces an upper motor neuron syndrome — spastic paralysis, exaggerated tendon jerks, and an extensor plantar (Babinski) response — on the CONTRALATERAL side of the body, not the same side, and not the flaccid paralysis or reaction of degeneration characteristic of a lower motor neuron lesion.

## explicit_objective
State that an internal capsule lesion produces contralateral spastic hemiplegia, an upper motor neuron syndrome, not ipsilateral or flaccid paralysis.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Internal capsule lesion syndrome

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Internal capsule lesion|Spastic hemiplegia|Upper motor neuron syndrome

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing the internal capsule lesion's motor deficit on the SAME side as the lesion, or describing it as flaccid/lower motor neuron in character — internal capsule lesions are upper motor neuron lesions causing CONTRALATERAL spastic paralysis.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-04F7F764A6D679

## label
The basilar artery supplies the brainstem, so its occlusion causes brainstem ischemia

## canonical_key
basilarartery.territory.brainstem-ischemia

## definition
The basilar artery, formed by the union of the two vertebral arteries at the pontomedullary junction, runs along the ventral surface of the pons giving off paramedian and circumferential branches that supply the pons and midbrain, before terminating as the two posterior cerebral arteries. Occlusion of the basilar artery or its branches therefore produces brainstem ischemia, with a range of syndromes depending on the level and branch involved (from isolated cranial nerve/long tract signs to devastating locked-in syndrome from a complete basilar occlusion), distinct from the internal or external carotid artery territories (cerebral hemispheres and face/scalp respectively) or the lingual artery (tongue and floor of mouth).

## explicit_objective
State that the basilar artery supplies the brainstem (pons and midbrain), so its occlusion produces brainstem ischemia.

## concept_type
definition

## support_mode

## subject

## subtopic
Basilar artery territory

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Basilar artery|Brainstem ischemia|Posterior circulation

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the basilar artery's brainstem territory with the internal carotid artery's cerebral hemisphere territory, or with the external carotid/lingual arteries' extracranial territories.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C45E0A9DC87329

## label
Sternocleidomastoid is spared in facial nerve (LMN) palsy

## canonical_key
neuro.facial-nerve.sternomastoid-spared-in-palsy

## definition
The facial nerve (CN VII) supplies the muscles of facial expression, stapedius, buccinator, orbicularis oculi and other second-arch derivatives. Sternocleidomastoid is a spinal accessory nerve (CN XI) muscle, not a facial-nerve muscle, so it is spared even in a complete lower motor neuron facial nerve palsy (Bell's palsy).

## explicit_objective
State that sternocleidomastoid, being an accessory-nerve (CN XI) muscle, is spared in facial nerve palsy, unlike buccinator and orbicularis oculi.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-AA223B5B388120

## label
This source's Workshop Quiz 3 marks the olfactory nerve's functional component as SVE, though standard teaching classifies it as SVA

## canonical_key
neuro.cranial-nerve.olfactory-nerve-functional-component

## definition
The seven functional-component scheme classifies cranial nerve fibers as GSE/GVE/SVE (motor) or GSA/GVA/SVA/SSA (sensory). The olfactory nerve (CN I) is a purely sensory nerve arising from the olfactory epithelium, and standard neuroanatomy teaching (its embryological kinship with the special-visceral-afferent taste pathway) classifies it as SVA (special visceral afferent) — SVE is a motor category that applies to branchial-arch muscles (e.g. facial, trigeminal, vagus, accessory), which cannot describe a purely sensory nerve.

## explicit_objective
State that the olfactory nerve is a purely sensory (SVA) cranial nerve, distinct from the SVE (branchiomotor) category used for the trigeminal, facial, glossopharyngeal, vagus and accessory nerves.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Functional component classification

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Olfactory nerve classification|SVA vs SVE|Cranial nerve functional components

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing SVA (special visceral afferent, the olfactory/taste sensory category) with SVE (special visceral efferent, the branchiomotor category), or assigning a purely sensory nerve a motor functional component.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4B44D513B80927

## label
The parieto-temporo-occipital junction ("circular area"), when destroyed, causes word blindness (alexia)

## canonical_key
neuro.cerebral-cortex.circular-area-word-blindness

## definition
The angular gyrus and the surrounding parieto-temporo-occipital junction — sometimes drawn on teaching diagrams as a border-zone "circular area" around the lateral (Sylvian) fissure's posterior end — integrate visual, auditory and somatosensory association cortex for reading and writing. A lesion here classically produces alexia (word blindness, inability to read written language) and, when it extends to include agraphia, forms part of the angular gyrus (Gerstmann-adjacent) syndrome, distinct from Broca's aphasia (expressive, inferior frontal) or cortical deafness (auditory cortex).

## explicit_objective
Attribute word blindness (alexia) to destruction of the parieto-temporo-occipital junction/angular gyrus region, distinct from Broca's aphasia or hearing loss.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Cerebral cortex

## subtopic
Language and reading cortex

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Circular area|Angular gyrus lesion|Alexia|Word blindness

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing alexia (a reading disorder from a parieto-temporo-occipital lesion) with Broca's aphasia (an expressive-speech disorder from an inferior frontal lesion) or with a primary hearing deficit.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-FE4E8E91C5BFFB

## label
The middle cerebral artery arises as the larger terminal branch of the internal carotid artery

## canonical_key
neuro.cerebral-vasculature.mca-origin-from-ica

## definition
At the base of the brain, the internal carotid artery terminates by dividing into two terminal branches: the smaller anterior cerebral artery and the much larger middle cerebral artery (MCA), which continues the ICA's general trajectory laterally into the Sylvian fissure. The MCA is not a branch of the posterior cerebral or basilar arteries, which instead arise from the vertebrobasilar (posterior circulation) system.

## explicit_objective
State that the middle cerebral artery is the internal carotid artery's larger terminal branch, distinct from the posterior circulation's basilar/posterior cerebral arteries.

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning the MCA to the posterior (vertebrobasilar) circulation rather than as the ICA's own terminal continuation.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4A0F99727B2E94

## label
The amygdaloid nucleus bulges into and compresses the temporal (inferior) horn of the lateral ventricle

## canonical_key
neuro.temporal-lobe.amygdala-compresses-temporal-horn

## definition
The amygdala sits in the medial temporal lobe at the anterior tip of the temporal (inferior) horn of the lateral ventricle, together with the hippocampus that lines its floor. Its bulging medial surface indents and narrows the temporal horn — a landmark used in reading temporal-lobe imaging — distinct from the 3rd or 4th ventricles, which lie in the midline diencephalon and brainstem/cerebellum respectively, well away from the amygdala.

## explicit_objective
State that the amygdaloid nucleus bulges into and compresses the temporal horn of the lateral ventricle, not the 3rd or 4th ventricle.

definition

## status

## support_mode

## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Amygdala|Temporal horn of lateral ventricle

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing the amygdala's ventricular relation at the 3rd or 4th ventricle rather than the temporal horn of the lateral ventricle, where it actually sits alongside the hippocampus.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-9D8B5F9A96EFEB

## label
The facial colliculus swelling in the 4th ventricle floor is formed by the abducens nucleus, looped over by facial nerve fibers

## canonical_key
neuro.brainstem.facial-colliculus-abducens-nucleus

## definition
In the pons, the facial nerve's intrapontine fibers loop (the internal genu) around the abducens (CN VI) nucleus before exiting the brainstem, and this looped complex bulges the floor of the fourth ventricle to form the facial colliculus. Despite its name, the colliculus's underlying swelling is produced chiefly by the abducens nucleus, not a facial nucleus that sits at that level — the facial motor nucleus itself lies more ventrolaterally in the pontine tegmentum.

## explicit_objective
State that the facial colliculus is formed principally by the abducens nucleus (with the looping facial nerve fibers around it), not by a facial nucleus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic
Brainstem

## subtopic
Fourth ventricle floor landmarks

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Facial colliculus|Abducens nucleus|Internal genu of facial nerve

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming the facial colliculus is formed by the facial nucleus because of its name, rather than by the abducens nucleus that the facial nerve's internal genu loops around.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-71F37542DF2271

## label
Vagus nerve rootlets emerge from the postolivary sulcus, between the olive and the inferior cerebellar peduncle

## canonical_key
neuro.cranial-nerve.vagus-rootlets-postolivary-sulcus

## definition
The vagus nerve's rootlets, together with the glossopharyngeal and cranial-root accessory nerve rootlets, emerge from the medulla's postolivary sulcus — the groove between the olive anterolaterally and the inferior cerebellar peduncle posterolaterally. This is distinct from the hypoglossal nerve's rootlets, which instead emerge from the preolivary sulcus, between the pyramid and the olive.

## explicit_objective
State that vagus nerve rootlets emerge from the postolivary sulcus, between the olive and inferior cerebellar peduncle, distinct from the hypoglossal nerve's preolivary emergence.

definition

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing vagus nerve rootlets in the preolivary sulcus (the hypoglossal nerve's site) rather than the postolivary sulcus, between the olive and inferior cerebellar peduncle.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C8E29E775C6856

## label
The maxillary nerve (V2) supplies sensation to the upper lip

## canonical_key
neuro.cranial-nerve.maxillary-nerve-upper-lip

## definition
The maxillary division of the trigeminal nerve (V2) supplies cutaneous and mucosal sensation to the mid-face, including the upper lip, via its infraorbital nerve branch. This is distinct from the ophthalmic division (V1, upper face/eye) and the mandibular division (V3, lower face/chin/lower lip), so an isolated upper-lip sensory lesion localises to V2.

## explicit_objective
Localise upper-lip sensory loss to the maxillary (V2) division of the trigeminal nerve, distinct from the ophthalmic and mandibular divisions.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## primary_node_id

## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Maxillary nerve|Infraorbital nerve|Trigeminal divisions

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning upper-lip sensation to the ophthalmic (V1) or mandibular (V3) division rather than the maxillary (V2) division, whose infraorbital branch supplies it.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4D96D26186D63D

## label
This source's Workshop Quiz 3 marks the dorsal nucleus of the vagus as supplying the lacrimal gland, though standard teaching attributes lacrimal parasympathetic supply to the superior salivatory nucleus

## canonical_key
neuro.autonomic.superior-salivatory-nucleus-lacrimal-gland

## definition
Standard physiology teaching places the lacrimal gland's parasympathetic supply in the superior salivatory nucleus, whose preganglionic fibers travel via the facial nerve's greater petrosal nerve and synapse in the pterygopalatine ganglion before reaching the gland — the same nucleus also supplies the submandibular and sublingual glands via the chorda tympani. The dorsal (motor) nucleus of the vagus, by contrast, supplies parasympathetic outflow to thoracic and abdominal viscera (heart, lungs, gut to the splenic flexure), not the lacrimal gland.

## explicit_objective
State that the superior salivatory nucleus, not the dorsal nucleus of the vagus, is the parasympathetic nucleus supplying the lacrimal gland.

definition

under review

## support_mode

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Attributing lacrimal gland parasympathetic supply to the dorsal (vagal) nucleus, a thoracoabdominal-viscera nucleus, rather than the superior salivatory nucleus; see author_notes for this source's own printed key.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B657A9987411BF

## label
The lateral motor nucleus (limb-muscle motor column) is not found in thoracic spinal cord segments

## canonical_key
neuro.spinal-cord.thoracic-lateral-motor-nucleus-absent

## definition
Thoracic spinal cord anterior horn grey matter genuinely contains the medial motor nucleus (axial/trunk muscles) and, in the lateral horn, the sympathetic (intermediolateral) nucleus and Clark's nucleus. The lateral motor nucleus, which supplies limb muscles, is instead concentrated in the cervical and lumbosacral enlargements (the segments innervating the upper and lower limbs), and is essentially absent from the thoracic segments, which have no limb musculature to supply.

## explicit_objective
State that the lateral (limb) motor nucleus is absent from thoracic spinal cord segments, unlike the medial motor, sympathetic and Clark's nuclei, which are genuinely thoracic.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Lateral motor nucleus|Thoracic cord grey matter|Limb motor column

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming every named ventral-horn motor nucleus is present at every cord level; the lateral (limb) motor column is confined to the cervical and lumbosacral enlargements, absent thoracically.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E52F57F2921C77

## label
Middle meningeal vessels pass through the foramen spinosum

## canonical_key
neuro.skull-base.foramen-spinosum-middle-meningeal

## definition
The middle meningeal artery and vein pass through the foramen spinosum, a small opening in the greater wing of the sphenoid posteromedial to foramen ovale. This is distinct from foramen ovale (mandibular nerve), foramen lacerum (largely closed by cartilage, transmitting only small vessels) and foramen rotundum (maxillary nerve).

## explicit_objective
State that the middle meningeal vessels pass through the foramen spinosum, distinct from the mandibular nerve's foramen ovale or the maxillary nerve's foramen rotundum.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-6741DFE8920A3E

## label
The palatine tonsil originates from the second pharyngeal pouch

## canonical_key
embryology.pharyngeal-pouch.palatine-tonsil-second-pouch

## definition
The palatine tonsil develops from the second pharyngeal pouch's endodermal lining, which is invaded by lymphoid tissue to form the tonsillar crypts and follicles. This is distinct from the first pouch (middle ear/auditory tube), third pouch (inferior parathyroid, thymus) and fourth pouch (superior parathyroid, ultimobranchial body).

## explicit_objective
State that the palatine tonsil originates from the second pharyngeal pouch, distinct from the first, third or fourth pouches' derivatives.

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning the palatine tonsil to the first, third or fourth pharyngeal pouch rather than the second, whose endoderm it develops from.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-70BDEFD335E3E1

## label
Oligodendrocytes are not derived from the neural crest

## canonical_key
embryology.neural-crest.oligodendrocyte-not-neural-crest

## definition
Oligodendrocytes, the CNS myelinating glia, arise from the neuroepithelium of the neural tube, not the neural crest. Schwann cells (PNS myelinating glia) and the pia mater (leptomeninges), by contrast, ARE neural-crest derivatives, along with pharyngeal-arch ectomesenchyme — making oligodendrocytes the exception among these four options.

## explicit_objective
State that oligodendrocytes are neural-tube (not neural-crest) derivatives, unlike Schwann cells, pia mater and pharyngeal-arch mesenchyme.

Oligodendrocytes|Neural crest derivatives|Schwann cells

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming all glial cells share a neural-crest origin; oligodendrocytes (CNS) are neuroepithelial, while Schwann cells (PNS) are neural-crest derivatives.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-24E57A0FBCE877

## label
The ciliary body regulates lens accommodation

## canonical_key
neuro.eye.ciliary-body-accommodation-b-label

## definition
The ciliary body, via the ciliary muscle acting through the zonular fibers on the lens capsule, is the structure responsible for accommodation — adjusting lens curvature/power for near vision. This is distinct from the trabecular meshwork (aqueous humor outflow, site of blockage in glaucoma) and the ciliary epithelium's separate secretory role (aqueous humor production), both of which are anatomically related but functionally distinct roles of nearby structures.

## explicit_objective
Identify the ciliary body/ciliary muscle as the structure regulating accommodation, distinct from the aqueous humor outflow and production roles of neighbouring structures.

definition

## status

## support_mode

## modules


## aliases
Ciliary body|Accommodation|Ciliary muscle

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Conflating the ciliary body's accommodation role with the trabecular meshwork's aqueous outflow role or the ciliary epithelium's aqueous production role — three distinct functions of related anterior-segment structures.

# Item

## id
CON-FND-D33FD145EF1078

## label
The sixth pharyngeal arch cartilage gives rise to the arytenoid, cricoid and corniculate cartilages

## canonical_key
embryology.pharyngeal-arch.sixth-arch-cartilage-derivatives

## definition
The sixth pharyngeal arch cartilage forms the arytenoid, cricoid and corniculate cartilages of the larynx, together with the cricothyroid muscle's antagonist intrinsic laryngeal muscles (supplied by the recurrent laryngeal nerve). The thyroid cartilage instead derives from the fourth arch, and the greater horn of the hyoid bone from the third arch — distinct pharyngeal-arch cartilage assignments that are commonly tested against each other.

## explicit_objective
State that the arytenoid cartilage is a sixth-arch derivative, distinct from the fourth arch's thyroid cartilage and the third arch's greater horn of the hyoid.

## arabic_aliases

Assigning the arytenoid cartilage to the fourth arch (thyroid cartilage's arch) or confusing it with the third arch's greater horn of the hyoid.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4962859DA23764

## label
The lateral horns of spinal cord grey matter contain sympathetic (and sacral parasympathetic) preganglionic nuclei

## canonical_key
neuro.spinal-cord.lateral-horn-sympathetic-nuclei

## definition
The lateral horn, present at thoracic and upper lumbar (T1-L2) segments and again at sacral (S2-4) segments, contains the intermediolateral cell column — sympathetic preganglionic neurons at T1-L2 and parasympathetic preganglionic neurons at S2-4. This is distinct from the anterior horn's motor (somatic efferent) nuclei and the posterior horn's sensory relay nuclei, which are not classified as lateral-horn structures.

## explicit_objective
State that the lateral horn of spinal grey matter houses autonomic (sympathetic T1-L2, parasympathetic S2-4) preganglionic nuclei, distinct from anterior-horn motor or posterior-horn sensory nuclei.

definition

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Lateral horn|Intermediolateral cell column|Sympathetic preganglionic neurons

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning motor or sensory function to the lateral horn, which is instead the spinal cord's autonomic (sympathetic/sacral parasympathetic) preganglionic column.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-FEDCD802906A53

## label
The cerebello-reticular tract exits the cerebellum via the inferior cerebellar peduncle

## canonical_key
neuro.cerebellum.inferior-peduncle-efferent-tracts

## definition
The inferior cerebellar peduncle (restiform body) carries predominantly afferent input into the cerebellum (dorsal spinocerebellar, olivocerebellar, cuneocerebellar, vestibulocerebellar fibers), but also carries a smaller efferent component leaving the cerebellum — including cerebello-vestibular and cerebello-reticular fibers, which project from the fastigial nucleus to the vestibular nuclei and reticular formation respectively. Corticopontocerebellar fibers, by contrast, enter via the MIDDLE cerebellar peduncle, which is purely afferent.

## explicit_objective
Identify the cerebello-reticular tract as an efferent pathway leaving the cerebellum via the inferior cerebellar peduncle, alongside its predominantly afferent traffic.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Cerebellar peduncle connections

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Inferior cerebellar peduncle|Cerebello-reticular tract|Restiform body

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assuming the inferior cerebellar peduncle is purely afferent; it also carries a genuine efferent component (cerebello-vestibular, cerebello-reticular fibers) out of the cerebellum.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-4E16F054159EF4

## label
The internal medullary lamina is the Y-shaped white matter sheet dividing the thalamus into anterior, medial and lateral nuclear groups

## canonical_key
neuro.thalamus.internal-medullary-lamina

## definition
The internal medullary lamina is a Y-shaped (or vertically-oriented) sheet of myelinated fibers within the thalamus that partitions its grey matter into the anterior, medial and lateral nuclear groups, and splits anteriorly to enclose the anterior nuclear group. This is distinct from the lamina terminalis (a diencephalic midline structure, not intrathalamic) and the stria medullaris thalami (a fiber bundle on the thalamus's dorsomedial surface, not an internal dividing sheet).

## explicit_objective
Identify the internal medullary lamina as the Y-shaped fiber sheet dividing the thalamus into anterior, medial and lateral nuclear groups.

definition

Internal medullary lamina|Thalamic nuclear groups|Stria medullaris thalami

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Confusing the internal medullary lamina (the intrathalamic Y-shaped dividing sheet) with the stria medullaris thalami or lamina terminalis, which are different structures entirely.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E82953158F217C

## label
The posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA) shares in the blood supply of the medulla

## canonical_key
neuro.brainstem-vasculature.pica-medulla-supply

## definition
The posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA), typically a branch of the vertebral artery, supplies the dorsolateral medulla (along with the inferior cerebellar surface) — its occlusion produces the lateral medullary (Wallenberg) syndrome. This is distinct from the superior cerebellar artery (midbrain/pons, branch of the basilar) and the posterior cerebral/middle cerebral arteries, which supply cerebral hemispheric territory rather than the medulla.

## explicit_objective
State that the posterior inferior cerebellar artery supplies the medulla, distinct from the superior cerebellar, posterior cerebral or middle cerebral arteries.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-6908C5B1242821

## label
Sternocleidomastoid bends the head to the same (ipsilateral) side and rotates the face to the opposite (contralateral) side

## canonical_key
neuro.cranial-nerve.sternocleidomastoid-action

## definition
Unilateral contraction of sternocleidomastoid produces lateral flexion (bending) of the head toward the SAME side as the contracting muscle, combined with rotation of the face toward the OPPOSITE side — the combined action used clinically to test accessory nerve (CN XI) function. This is distinct from trapezius (shoulder shrug/scapular rotation), scalenus medius (rib elevation/lateral neck flexion without the same rotary component) or buccinator (cheek compression, facial nerve).

## explicit_objective
State that sternocleidomastoid produces ipsilateral head tilt with contralateral face rotation, the muscle's classic combined action.

## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Reversing sternocleidomastoid's action (contralateral tilt/ipsilateral rotation) rather than the correct ipsilateral-tilt/contralateral-rotation combination.

## label
The spinal accessory nerve (CN XI) motor nucleus is classified as SVE (special visceral efferent), the branchiomotor category

## canonical_key
neuro.cranial-nerve.accessory-nerve-sve-classification

## definition
The cranial nerves supplying muscles derived embryologically from the pharyngeal (branchial) arches — trigeminal, facial, glossopharyngeal, vagus and the cranial/spinal accessory nerve (sternocleidomastoid and trapezius) — share the SVE (special visceral efferent, branchiomotor) functional component, distinct from GSE (somatic motor, e.g. hypoglossal, oculomotor) and GVE (general visceral motor/autonomic) categories.

## explicit_objective
State that the accessory nerve's motor nucleus, supplying sternocleidomastoid and trapezius, is classified SVE (branchiomotor), like the trigeminal, facial, glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-5829C2A51C495F

## label
The precuneus is supplied chiefly by the anterior cerebral artery

## canonical_key
neuro.cerebral-vasculature.precuneus-aca-supply

## definition
The precuneus, on the medial surface of the parietal lobe between the marginal (cingulate) sulcus and the parieto-occipital sulcus, lies in an anterior-cerebral-artery / posterior-cerebral-artery border zone but is most often attributed to the pericallosal/distal branches of the anterior cerebral artery (ACA) in standard vascular-territory teaching. This distinguishes it from the middle cerebral artery, which supplies the lateral convexity, and the vertebral artery, which supplies the posterior fossa/brainstem.

## explicit_objective
State that the precuneus's blood supply is chiefly attributed to the anterior cerebral artery, at the medial parietal ACA/PCA border zone.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Precuneus|Anterior cerebral artery|Medial parietal cortex

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning the precuneus purely to the posterior or middle cerebral artery territory, without recognising its border-zone position and the anterior cerebral artery's own contribution.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-7E62EDEAA1FAAA

## label
The posterior superior alveolar nerve supplies sensation to the upper molar teeth (excluding the mesiobuccal root of the first molar)

## canonical_key
neuro.cranial-nerve.posterior-superior-alveolar-nerve

## definition
The posterior superior alveolar nerve, a branch of the maxillary nerve (V2), supplies sensory innervation to the maxillary molar teeth (most reliably the second and third/'last' molars) and the overlying gingiva and maxillary sinus mucosa. This is distinct from the anterior superior alveolar nerve (incisors/canine), the middle superior alveolar nerve (premolars and the first molar's mesiobuccal root, when present), and the inferior alveolar nerve (V3, mandibular teeth).

## explicit_objective
State that the posterior superior alveolar nerve supplies sensation to the upper (maxillary) molar teeth, distinct from the anterior/middle superior alveolar or inferior alveolar nerves.

definition

## microtopic

Posterior superior alveolar nerve|Maxillary molar sensation|Trigeminal dental branches

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Assigning upper-molar (especially last-molar) sensation to the middle superior alveolar or inferior alveolar nerve rather than the posterior superior alveolar nerve.
