# Item

## id
CON-NEU-27210A54963FBF

## label
Paramedian branches of the basilar artery supply the pons via the basilar sulcus

## canonical_key
neuro.brainstem-vasculature.basilar-artery-pontine-branch

## definition
The basilar artery, formed by the union of the two vertebral arteries at the pontomedullary junction, runs along the midline basilar sulcus on the pons's ventral surface, giving off paramedian and short/long circumferential branches that supply the pons and much of the midbrain. This distinguishes an artery running in this specific groove from the internal or external carotid arteries (anterior circulation) or the lingual artery (a branch of the external carotid supplying the tongue, unrelated to brainstem sulci).

## explicit_objective
State that the artery running in the pons's basilar sulcus is the basilar artery, supplying the pons via paramedian branches.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids


## topic

## subtopic
Basilar artery and pontine supply

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Basilar artery|Basilar sulcus|Pontine blood supply

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Attributing the vessel running in the pons's midline basilar sulcus to the carotid or lingual arteries rather than the basilar artery, the posterior-circulation vessel that actually occupies that groove.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-3D4ADCAFC8B82E

## label
The substantia nigra is located in the midbrain

## canonical_key
neuro.midbrain.substantia-nigra-location

## definition
The substantia nigra, a dopaminergic nucleus of the basal ganglia system, is located throughout the midbrain (mesencephalon), extending from its upper to lower limits — its degeneration underlies Parkinson disease. This is distinct from the pons or medulla oblongata, and broader than a narrow "superior midbrain only" placement.

## explicit_objective
State that the substantia nigra is a midbrain structure, spanning its extent rather than being confined to the upper pons or a narrow superior-midbrain slice.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic
Substantia nigra location

## microtopic


## nanotopic


## modules


## aliases
Substantia nigra|Midbrain|Parkinson disease pathology

## arabic_label


## arabic_aliases


## pitfalls
Placing the substantia nigra in the pons or medulla, or restricting it to only the most superior part of the midbrain, rather than recognising it spans the midbrain's extent.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-8FABD01DF54147

## label
Occipitalis has a bony attachment and is supplied by the posterior auricular branch of the facial nerve

## canonical_key
anatomy.occipitofrontalis.occipital-belly-bony-attachment-posterior-auricular

## definition
Occipitofrontalis is a single muscle with two bellies joined by the galea aponeurotica: the frontal belly (frontalis) has NO bony attachment, blending into the skin/eyebrow superiorly and supplied by the TEMPORAL branch of the facial nerve, while the occipital belly (occipitalis) DOES have a bony attachment — arising from the highest nuchal line of the occipital bone and the mastoid part of the temporal bone — and is supplied by the POSTERIOR AURICULAR branch of the facial nerve.

## explicit_objective
Distinguish the frontal belly (no bony attachment, temporal branch) from the occipital belly (bony attachment, posterior auricular branch) of occipitofrontalis.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Scalp and face

## subtopic
Occipitofrontalis innervation and attachment

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Occipitofrontalis muscle|Occipitalis nerve supply|Frontalis vs occipitalis

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming both bellies of occipitofrontalis share the same attachment pattern — only the occipital belly has a bony origin (occipital bone/mastoid); the frontal belly has none, ending in skin and the orbicularis oculi/eyebrow region instead.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-8FD623568F7735

## label
Loss of forehead wrinkling points to injury of the temporal branch of the facial nerve

## canonical_key
anatomy.facial-nerve.temporal-branch-forehead-wrinkling-loss

## definition
The frontal belly of occipitofrontalis (frontalis), responsible for wrinkling the forehead skin and raising the eyebrows, is supplied by the TEMPORAL branch of the facial nerve (CN VII). Injury to this branch — as from trauma along its superficial course over the zygomatic arch — paralyses frontalis, producing a smooth, unwrinkled forehead and inability to raise the eyebrow on the affected side, a classic peripheral (lower motor neuron) facial nerve finding.

## explicit_objective
State that inability to wrinkle the forehead localises to injury of the temporal branch of the facial nerve.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic
Facial nerve — temporal branch and forehead wrinkling

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Temporal branch facial nerve|Frontalis paralysis|Forehead wrinkling loss

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing which facial nerve branch supplies frontalis (temporal branch) with other named branches (zygomatic, buccal, marginal mandibular, cervical) that supply different facial muscle groups.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-971BBB6832830A

## label
Scalp avulsion classically separates through the fourth (loose areolar) layer

## canonical_key
anatomy.scalp.avulsion-plane-fourth-loose-areolar-layer

## definition
The scalp has five layers, remembered by the mnemonic SCALP: Skin, dense subcutaneous Connective tissue, galea Aponeurotica, Loose areolar tissue, and Pericranium. The loose areolar (fourth) layer is a plane of easy cleavage between the aponeurosis above and the pericranium below, which is exactly why traumatic scalp avulsion ('scalping') injuries classically separate through this fourth layer, tearing the first three layers away as a relatively clean unit.

## explicit_objective
State that traumatic scalp separation (avulsion) classically occurs through the fourth (loose areolar) layer.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Scalp and face

## subtopic
Scalp layer of surgical/traumatic separation

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Scalp avulsion|Loose areolar layer|SCALP mnemonic layers

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the loose areolar (danger) layer, the classic plane of scalp avulsion, with the dense connective tissue (second) layer, which is instead the classic site of profuse bleeding when injured because it firmly binds blood vessels open.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-9AE148EB72A3CA

## label
The tentorium cerebelli is the intermediate dural fold between falx cerebri and falx cerebelli

## canonical_key
anatomy.dural-folds.tentorium-cerebelli-intermediate-fold

## definition
The dura mater forms four major reflected folds: falx cerebri (in the midline sagittal plane, between the cerebral hemispheres), falx cerebelli (in the midline, between the cerebellar hemispheres), tentorium cerebelli (a roughly horizontal fold separating the cerebrum above from the cerebellum below), and diaphragma sellae (roofing the pituitary fossa). Of these, the tentorium cerebelli sits anatomically and functionally between the two vertical falx folds — falx cerebri attaches to its upper surface anteriorly and falx cerebelli to its lower surface posteriorly — making it the intermediate fold linking the two.

## explicit_objective
State that the tentorium cerebelli is the intermediate dural fold between falx cerebri and falx cerebelli.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Meninges

## subtopic
Dural folds — tentorium cerebelli as the intermediate fold

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Tentorium cerebelli|Dural fold arrangement|Intermediate dural septum

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the four dural folds' relative positions — only the tentorium cerebelli is horizontally oriented and interposed between the two vertical midline folds (falx cerebri above, falx cerebelli below).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-9E8165F7FCF947

## label
The free margin of the tentorium cerebelli attaches anteriorly to the anterior clinoid processes

## canonical_key
anatomy.tentorium-cerebelli.free-margin-anterior-clinoid-process-attachment

## definition
The tentorium cerebelli has two margins: a peripheral (attached, convex) margin fixed to the skull along the transverse sulcus, petrous ridge and posterior clinoid processes, and a free (concave) margin that bounds the tentorial notch (incisura) through which the midbrain passes. This free margin attaches ANTERIORLY, on each side, to the ANTERIOR CLINOID PROCESS of the sphenoid bone.

## explicit_objective
State that the free margin of the tentorium cerebelli attaches anteriorly to the anterior clinoid process.

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls

## label
The straight sinus is formed by union of the inferior sagittal sinus and the great cerebral vein

## canonical_key
anatomy.straight-sinus.formed-by-iss-and-great-cerebral-vein

## definition
The straight sinus runs along the line where falx cerebri meets the upper surface of the tentorium cerebelli, formed at its anterior end by the union of the INFERIOR SAGITTAL SINUS (draining the falx cerebri and medial cerebral surfaces) with the GREAT CEREBRAL VEIN OF GALEN (draining the deep structures of the brain); it then runs posteriorly to drain into the confluence of sinuses.

## explicit_objective

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A2EC1AA72BE7CF

## label
Cavernous sinus thrombosis classically produces squint, exophthalmos and can progress to death

## canonical_key
anatomy.cavernous-sinus-thrombosis.squint-exophthalmos-death-triad

## definition
Cavernous sinus thrombosis is a life-threatening condition, most often arising from spread of facial or paranasal sinus infection via the valveless veins draining into the cavernous sinus. Its classic features include ophthalmoplegia/SQUINT (from compression of the oculomotor, trochlear and abducent nerves in the lateral wall/body of the sinus), proptosis/EXOPHTHALMOS and chemosis (from impaired venous drainage of the orbit via the ophthalmic veins), fever, and — if untreated — progression to intracranial spread, meningitis and DEATH.

## explicit_objective
List squint, exophthalmos and death among the recognised features/outcomes of cavernous sinus thrombosis.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Cavernous sinus thrombosis complications

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Cavernous sinus thrombosis|CST complications|Septic cavernous sinus thrombosis

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Treating this exam item's exclusion of 'blindness' as a claim that visual loss never occurs in cavernous sinus thrombosis — clinically, visual impairment IS a recognised (though typically later or less emphasised in this exam's framing) complication, via optic nerve/retinal venous congestion; the printed key is kept as authored, with this divergence flagged.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-A5A90309AED2E6

## label
The transverse sinus runs along the tentorium cerebelli’s attachment to the occipital bone

## canonical_key
anatomy.tentorium-cerebelli.transverse-sinus-at-occipital-attachment

## definition
Along its attached (peripheral) margin, the tentorium cerebelli carries different named venous sinuses depending on which bone it is fixed to: where it attaches to the occipital bone (along the transverse sulcus/groove), it carries the TRANSVERSE SINUS, while where it attaches to the superior border of the petrous temporal bone it instead carries the superior petrosal sinus.

## explicit_objective
State that the transverse sinus runs in the tentorium cerebelli's attachment to the occipital bone.

## microtopic

Transverse sinus|Tentorium occipital attachment|Dural venous sinus groove

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the transverse sinus (tentorium's attachment to the occipital bone) with the superior petrosal sinus (tentorium's attachment to the petrous temporal bone) — both run within the tentorium's attached margin, but to different bones.

CON-NEU-ABA48C0650255E

## label
The trochlear nerve pierces the dura at the point where the tentorium’s free and attached margins cross

## canonical_key
anatomy.trochlear-nerve.dural-entry-at-free-attached-margin-crossing

## definition
The trochlear nerve (CN IV), after emerging from the dorsal midbrain and winding around the cerebral peduncle, is the only cranial nerve classically taught to pierce the dura mater specifically at the point where the tentorium cerebelli's FREE margin crosses its ATTACHED (peripheral) margin, posterolateral to the posterior clinoid process, before running forward within the lateral wall of the cavernous sinus.

## explicit_objective
State that the trochlear nerve pierces the dura at the point where the tentorium's free and attached margins cross.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Cranial nerves

## subtopic
Trochlear nerve dural entry point

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Trochlear nerve dural entry|Tentorium margin crossing|CN IV course

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming any of the other cavernous-sinus-related cranial nerves (III, V1, V2) share this specific dural entry point — the crossing of the tentorium's free and attached margins is a landmark specifically associated with the trochlear nerve's course.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-ACA5AD215D874E

## label
The facial nerve is not a content of the cavernous sinus, unlike the oculomotor nerve

## canonical_key
anatomy.cavernous-sinus.facial-nerve-not-a-content

## definition
The cavernous sinus's lateral wall carries, from superior to inferior, the oculomotor (III), trochlear (IV), ophthalmic (V1) and maxillary (V2) nerves, while the abducent nerve (VI) runs freely within the body of the sinus alongside the internal carotid artery. The facial nerve (CN VII) takes an entirely different course — through the internal acoustic meatus and facial canal to the stylomastoid foramen — and is NOT a content of the cavernous sinus at all.

## explicit_objective
State that the facial nerve, unlike the oculomotor nerve, is not a content of the cavernous sinus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Cavernous sinus contents|Facial nerve exclusion|Cranial nerves III-VI cavernous sinus

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming all cranial nerves running near the base of the skull pass through the cavernous sinus — only III, IV, V1, V2 (lateral wall) and VI (within the sinus body) do; VII takes a separate course through the temporal bone.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-AD3B1F5D56C906

## label
Dural venous sinuses lack muscle fibers in their walls, unlike ordinary veins

## canonical_key
anatomy.dural-venous-sinus.no-muscle-fibers-in-wall

## definition
Dural venous sinuses are endothelium-lined channels running between the two layers of the dura mater (the periosteal and meningeal layers), receiving venous blood from the brain, meninges, skull bones (diploic veins) and scalp (via emissary veins). Unlike ordinary veins, their rigid dural walls contain NO SMOOTH MUSCLE FIBERS, and they also have no valves — features that keep them permanently patent, non-collapsible conduits for cerebral venous drainage.

## explicit_objective
State that dural venous sinuses lack muscle fibers in their walls, unlike ordinary veins.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
General dural venous sinus features

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Dural venous sinus wall|No muscle fibers|No valves feature

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming dural venous sinuses share the same wall structure as peripheral veins — their rigid, dura-derived walls have no smooth muscle and no valves, unlike ordinary veins elsewhere in the body.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-ADBB66E6E97DFF

## label
The oculomotor nerve is anterior to the trochlear nerve at the tentorium cerebelli

## canonical_key
anatomy.tentorium-cerebelli.oculomotor-anterior-to-trochlear

## definition
As the oculomotor nerve (III) pierces the dura near the posterior clinoid process (through the oculomotor trigone between the tentorium's free margins) and the trochlear nerve (IV) pierces the dura more posterolaterally, at the crossing of the tentorium's free and attached margins, the OCULOMOTOR nerve's dural entry point — and its subsequent course in the lateral wall of the cavernous sinus — lies ANTERIOR to that of the trochlear nerve.

## explicit_objective
State that the oculomotor nerve is anterior to the trochlear nerve at the tentorium cerebelli.

## concept_type
definition

## status

## support_mode

## primary_node_id

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Oculomotor vs trochlear nerve position|Tentorial notch nerves|Cranial nerve III and IV relation

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the tentorium's free (concave) margin, which bounds the tentorial notch, with its peripheral (attached) margin — and confusing which nerve (III vs IV) enters more anteriorly at the tentorium.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-AE7060FCD707AB

## label
The straight sinus is an unpaired (midline) dural venous sinus, unlike the sphenoparietal, cavernous and transverse sinuses

## canonical_key
anatomy.dural-venous-sinus.straight-sinus-is-unpaired

## definition
Several dural venous sinuses are PAIRED (bilateral, one on each side): the sphenoparietal, cavernous, superior petrosal, inferior petrosal, transverse and sigmoid sinuses. The straight sinus, by contrast, is a single, UNPAIRED, midline channel — running along the midline junction of falx cerebri and the tentorium cerebelli — like the superior sagittal and occipital sinuses.

## explicit_objective
State that the straight sinus is unpaired, unlike the sphenoparietal, cavernous and transverse sinuses.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Dural venous sinuses

## subtopic
Paired vs unpaired dural venous sinuses

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Unpaired dural venous sinus|Straight sinus midline course|Paired venous sinuses

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming all dural venous sinuses are paired structures — the midline sinuses (superior sagittal, inferior sagittal, straight, occipital) are unpaired, while sinuses running along the paired dural folds' lateral attachments (sphenoparietal, cavernous, petrosal, transverse, sigmoid) are paired.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B0B3385AF7F14D

## label
The inferior sagittal sinus unites with the great cerebral vein to form the straight sinus

## canonical_key
anatomy.inferior-sagittal-sinus.unites-with-great-cerebral-vein

## definition
The inferior sagittal sinus runs within the free (lower) margin of falx cerebri, receiving venous drainage from the medial surfaces of the cerebral hemispheres, and increases in calibre as it passes POSTERIORLY (not anteriorly), before uniting at the back of the falx with the great cerebral vein of Galen to form the straight sinus. It is a single, UNPAIRED midline sinus, not a paired one.

## explicit_objective
State that the inferior sagittal sinus unites with the great cerebral vein to form the straight sinus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Inferior sagittal sinus|Falx cerebri free margin sinus|Straight sinus tributary

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming the inferior sagittal sinus increases in size as it runs anteriorly, or that it is a paired sinus — it in fact enlarges posteriorly and is an unpaired midline structure.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B123D909CFF636

## label
The anterior and posterior intercavernous sinuses are not themselves bilaterally paired structures

## canonical_key
anatomy.intercavernous-sinus.not-a-bilaterally-paired-structure

## definition
The anterior and posterior intercavernous sinuses are two midline venous channels running within the anterior and posterior borders of the diaphragma sellae, connecting the right and left cavernous sinuses to one another and, together with them, forming a venous ring around the pituitary gland called the circular sinus. Although there are two intercavernous sinuses (anterior and posterior), each is itself a single midline channel, not a bilaterally (left-right) PAIRED structure the way the cavernous sinuses themselves are.

## explicit_objective
State that the intercavernous sinuses are not themselves bilaterally paired structures, despite existing as anterior and posterior channels.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B1C6C40BD3A509

## label
The cavernous sinus is related to the internal carotid artery, and to the sphenoid bone/air sinus inferiorly and medially

## canonical_key
anatomy.cavernous-sinus.relations-ica-sphenoid-inferior-medial

## definition
The cavernous sinus, on either side of the pituitary fossa, has the internal carotid artery (with its sympathetic plexus and the abducent nerve) coursing through its body, and its inferior and medial relations are formed by the body of the sphenoid bone and its contained sphenoid air sinus — so all three named relations (ICA, inferior sphenoid bone/sinus, medial sphenoid bone/sinus) are individually correct descriptions of the cavernous sinus's surroundings.

## explicit_objective
State that the cavernous sinus is related to the internal carotid artery and, inferiorly and medially, to the sphenoid bone and its air sinus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Cavernous sinus relations

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Cavernous sinus relations|Internal carotid artery in cavernous sinus|Sphenoid air sinus relation

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Treating the cavernous sinus's relation to the internal carotid artery as merely an adjacent, external 'superior' relation — the ICA in fact courses THROUGH the body of the sinus itself, alongside the abducent nerve, while III, IV, V1 and V2 run in its lateral wall.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-BCEB5307771256

## label
Emissary veins pass through the foramen ovale and foramen lacerum, among other skull foramina

## canonical_key
anatomy.emissary-veins.foramen-ovale-and-lacerum-pathways

## definition
Emissary veins are valveless veins connecting the extracranial (scalp/face) venous system to the intracranial dural venous sinuses, providing a potential route for extracranial infection to spread intracranially. Among the several skull foramina they traverse (including the parietal and mastoid foramina and the condylar canal), an emissary vein accompanies the mandibular nerve through the FORAMEN OVALE, and a further emissary channel is described passing through the FORAMEN LACERUM, connecting the cavernous sinus region to the pterygoid venous plexus.

## explicit_objective
State that emissary veins pass through the foramen ovale and foramen lacerum, among other skull foramina.

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C4B0A8489B265C

## label
This source keys the fourth (loose areolar) scalp layer as the vascular layer

## canonical_key
anatomy.scalp.fourth-layer-keyed-as-vascular-layer

## definition
Standard teaching describes the SECOND scalp layer (dense subcutaneous connective tissue) as the vascular layer, since it binds the scalp's numerous blood vessels open within its fibrous septa, producing profuse bleeding when this layer is injured; the fourth (loose areolar) layer is instead classically taught as the 'danger area', where blood or pus can track and spread to the eyelids via the frontalis/orbital connection, rather than being itself specifically named 'the vascular layer'. This item's printed key nonetheless selects the fourth layer as the vascular layer, a divergence from that standard framing that is kept per LANE-CARD and flagged here rather than silently resolved.

## explicit_objective
State this source's printed answer regarding which scalp layer is described as the vascular layer, while flagging the divergence from standard teaching.

definition

## status

## support_mode

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Scalp vascular layer|Loose areolar layer bleeding|Scalp layer divergent key

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Treating this item's printed key (fourth layer = vascular layer) as standard teaching without qualification — most anatomy texts instead describe the SECOND (dense connective tissue) layer as the vascular layer, with the fourth (loose areolar) layer known instead as the danger area for infection/haematoma tracking.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C867DCBD689779

## label
The supratrochlear and supraorbital nerves supply the scalp anterior to the auricle

## canonical_key
anatomy.scalp.supratrochlear-supraorbital-anterior-to-auricle-supply

## definition
Sensory innervation of the scalp is divided by a line roughly through the vertex: the region ANTERIOR to the auricle (forehead and anterior scalp) is supplied chiefly by branches of the ophthalmic (V1) division of the trigeminal nerve — the SUPRATROCHLEAR and SUPRAORBITAL nerves — together with the zygomaticotemporal nerve (V2) more laterally, while the region POSTERIOR to the auricle is supplied by cervical plexus branches (greater and lesser occipital, great auricular nerves), not by the trigeminal nerve.

## explicit_objective
State that the supratrochlear and supraorbital nerves supply the scalp anterior to the auricle.

## concept_type
definition

## status

## support_mode

## subject

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Supratrochlear nerve|Supraorbital nerve|Scalp sensory innervation

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming the trigeminal nerve supplies the scalp POSTERIOR to the auricle — that region is instead supplied by cervical plexus branches (occipital and great auricular nerves); the trigeminal nerve's supratrochlear/supraorbital branches supply the scalp ANTERIOR to the auricle.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F3FB27700BE4CF

## label
A dural fold is a reduplication of the meningeal (inner), not periosteal, layer of the dura

## canonical_key
anatomy.dural-fold.two-outer-dura-layers

## definition
The dura mater has two apposed layers: an outer periosteal layer, fused to the skull's inner table, and an inner meningeal layer. A dural fold such as the falx cerebri or tentorium cerebelli forms where the meningeal layer reduplicates (folds back on itself) and projects into the cranial cavity as a double septum, partitioning the intracranial compartments; the periosteal layer stays applied to bone and plays no part in the fold itself.

## explicit_objective
State that a dural fold is a reduplication of the meningeal (inner), not periosteal, layer of the dura.

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming a dural fold pairs the outer periosteal layer with the inner meningeal layer — both layers of a fold are the SAME (meningeal) layer folded back on itself; the periosteal layer stays lining the bone.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-55414891CA46FB

## label
The occipital sinus, carried by the falx cerebelli, is not related to the falx cerebri

## canonical_key
anatomy.dural-sinus.falx-cerebri-related-except-occipital

## definition
Three dural venous sinuses run along the borders of the falx cerebri: the superior sagittal sinus (superior, attached border), the inferior sagittal sinus (free, inferior border), and the straight sinus (posterior junction of the falx cerebri with the tentorium cerebelli). The occipital sinus, by contrast, runs in the attached margin of the FALX CEREBELLI, between the two layers of dura along the internal occipital crest, and drains into the confluence of sinuses — it is not carried by any margin of the falx cerebri.

## explicit_objective
Identify the occipital sinus, carried by the falx cerebelli, as the one venous sinus not related to the falx cerebri.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

direct_statement

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic
Sinuses carried by the falx cerebri

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Falx cerebri venous sinuses|Occipital sinus location|Superior and inferior sagittal sinus

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming every dural venous sinus with 'sagittal' or 'straight' in its name belongs to the falx cerebri, and by extension assuming the occipital sinus does too — it instead belongs to the falx cerebelli.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-182720E0F0F0C3

## label
The tentorium cerebelli's attached margin reaches the transverse sulcus

## canonical_key
anatomy.tentorium-cerebelli.attached-margin-transverse-sulcus

## definition
The tentorium cerebelli has two margins: a free (concave) margin bounding the tentorial notch, whose anterior attachment is the anterior clinoid process, and a peripheral (attached, convex) margin, fixed to the internal surface of the occipital and parietal bones and the upper border of the petrous temporal bone, following a line that meets the transverse sulcus (carrying the transverse sinus) posterolaterally.

## explicit_objective
State that the tentorium cerebelli's attached margin reaches the transverse sulcus, distinct from the free margin's anterior clinoid process attachment.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Tentorium cerebelli attachments|Transverse sulcus|Tentorial free vs attached margin

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the tentorium's free margin (anterior clinoid process) with its attached margin (transverse sulcus, along the occipital/parietal/petrous temporal bones) — the two margins have different bony attachments front to back.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-87308E84EC5296

## label
The cavernous sinus is the paired dural venous sinus among these four

## canonical_key
anatomy.dural-sinus.cavernous-sinus-is-paired

## definition
The cavernous sinus is a bilaterally paired structure, one on either side of the sella turcica, connected to its mate by the anterior and posterior intercavernous sinuses running within the borders of the diaphragma sellae. The occipital sinus, the intercavernous sinus (a midline connecting pair, not two separate lateral channels) and the basilar plexus of veins are each single midline venous structures, unlike the bilaterally duplicated cavernous sinus.

## explicit_objective
Identify the cavernous sinus as the paired dural venous sinus among these four, unlike the single occipital sinus, intercavernous sinus and basilar plexus.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## subtopic
Paired vs single dural venous sinuses

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Paired dural venous sinuses|Cavernous sinus laterality|Single vs paired sinuses

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Treating the 'intercavernous sinus' as itself a paired lateral structure — it is a single midline connecting channel (or pair of channels crossing the midline) linking the two cavernous sinuses, not a duplicated sinus in its own right.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-2877B4F8771424

## label
The internal carotid artery lies within, not superior to, the cavernous sinus

## canonical_key
anatomy.cavernous-sinus.relations-except-supraorbital-artery

## definition
The internal carotid artery (with its surrounding sympathetic plexus) runs freely WITHIN the body of the cavernous sinus, not related to it 'superiorly' from outside — superiorly, the sinus instead relates to the optic chiasm/hypophysis region. Inferiorly and medially, the sphenoid bone and its air sinus are genuine relations, separated from the sinus only by a thin bony plate.

## explicit_objective
Identify that the internal carotid artery lies within, not superior to, the cavernous sinus, unlike the sphenoid bone and air sinus, which are genuinely inferomedial relations.

definition

## status
under review

## support_mode

## nanotopic

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Describing the internal carotid artery as a superior relation of the cavernous sinus because it ascends near it — the artery actually runs freely within the body of the sinus, not superior to it.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-818A4F14FD6378

## label
The superior ophthalmic vein links the facial vein to the cavernous sinus

## canonical_key
anatomy.cavernous-sinus.communicates-facial-vein-superior-ophthalmic-vein

## definition
The superior ophthalmic vein connects the facial vein (via the angular vein at the medial canthus) to the cavernous sinus, forming a valveless communication between the extracranial facial venous system and an intracranial dural sinus. This is the anatomical basis by which facial and paranasal skin infections (e.g. from the nasolabial 'danger triangle') can spread retrogradely to cause cavernous sinus thrombosis.

## explicit_objective
State that the superior ophthalmic vein is the valveless conduit linking the facial vein to the cavernous sinus.

definition

## support_mode

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Attributing the facial-vein-to-cavernous-sinus communication to the inferior ophthalmic vein instead of the superior — the superior ophthalmic vein is the vessel that carries this specific communication.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-16C4408D38EC18

## label
The internal carotid artery, via a carotid-cavernous fistula, causes pulsating exophthalmos

## canonical_key
anatomy.cavernous-sinus.pulsating-exophthalmos-internal-carotid-artery

## definition
Pulsating exophthalmos results from a carotid-cavernous fistula: an abnormal direct communication between the internal carotid artery (running freely within the cavernous sinus) and the surrounding venous sinus, most often from trauma or aneurysm rupture. Arterial pressure is transmitted into the low-pressure venous sinus and its ophthalmic vein tributaries, producing pulsatile proptosis, a bruit, and chemosis.

## explicit_objective
Identify the internal carotid artery, via a carotid-cavernous fistula, as the vessel responsible for pulsating exophthalmos.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Carotid-cavernous fistula

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Carotid-cavernous fistula|Pulsating exophthalmos|Cavernous sinus arteriovenous communication

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing pulsating exophthalmos (carotid-cavernous fistula, an arteriovenous communication) with the ophthalmoplegia/proptosis of cavernous sinus thrombosis, which is a venous, not arterial, pathology.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-681FDB1E55A5A0

## label
The occipital artery does not supply scalp anterior to the auricle

## canonical_key
anatomy.scalp-artery.anterior-to-auricle-except-occipital

## definition
The scalp anterior to the auricle is supplied by the supratrochlear and supraorbital arteries (ophthalmic, internal carotid branches) and the superficial temporal artery (external carotid's terminal branch). The occipital artery, another external carotid branch, instead supplies the POSTERIOR scalp behind the auricle.

## explicit_objective
Identify the occipital artery as the one artery that does NOT supply the scalp anterior to the auricle, since it instead supplies the posterior scalp.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-EE9C1F8648A1C6

## label
This source's answer for anterior-scalp sensory loss: the zygomaticotemporal nerve

## canonical_key
anatomy.scalp-nerve.zygomaticotemporal-anterior-scalp

## definition
The scalp anterior to the auricle is trigeminal (V1/V2) territory. This source keys the zygomaticotemporal nerve, a maxillary (V2) branch running through the zygomatic bone to the temple, as the nerve whose injury produces anterior-scalp sensory loss — distinct from the cervical plexus nerves (lesser occipital, greater auricular, greater occipital) supplying the posterior scalp, and from the ophthalmic (V1) supratrochlear/supraorbital nerves supplying the scalp more medially.

## explicit_objective
Identify the zygomaticotemporal nerve, a trigeminal (V2) branch, as this source's answer for anterior-scalp sensory loss, distinct from the cervical plexus nerves supplying the posterior scalp.

definition

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Zygomaticotemporal nerve|Anterior scalp sensory loss|Trigeminal scalp territory

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Reaching for a cervical plexus nerve (great/lesser occipital, great auricular) for anterior scalp sensory loss — those nerves supply the posterior scalp; the anterior scalp is trigeminal territory.

Printed key retained per LANE-CARD.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-0A90A49264068F

## label
The orbital part of orbicularis oculi's protective, sustained closure action

## canonical_key
anatomy.orbicularis-oculi.orbital-part-action

## definition
Orbicularis oculi has three parts: orbital, palpebral and lacrimal. The orbital part, the muscle's outer, more powerful ring, produces a forceful, sustained squeeze that keeps the eyelids firmly closed — the part that stays tonically active during sleep and drives a forceful protective blink — unlike the palpebral part (the light, involuntary blink) and the lacrimal part (which pumps tears via the lacrimal sac).

## explicit_objective
State that the orbital part of orbicularis oculi's defining action is protective, forceful and sustained eyelid closure, including during sleep.

definition

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the orbital part (forceful, sustained closure/sleep) with the palpebral part (light, spontaneous blinking) — the two parts of orbicularis oculi serve different closure functions.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-B893B7C48F2154

## label
The facial nerve (CN VII) is the motor supply to the face

## canonical_key
anatomy.facial-muscle.motor-supply-cnvii

## definition
The facial nerve (CN VII), the second pharyngeal arch's own cranial nerve, is the motor nerve to every muscle of facial expression. The trigeminal nerve (CN V) instead provides facial sensation and motor supply to the muscles of mastication, and the accessory nerve (CN XI) supplies sternocleidomastoid and trapezius — neither substitutes for the facial nerve's role in facial movement.

## explicit_objective
State that the facial nerve (CN VII) is the motor supply to the face.

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the trigeminal nerve (facial SENSATION and mastication) with the facial nerve (facial MOTOR supply) — the two cranial nerves have opposite functional roles in the face.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F59B0F0B0797C9

## label
Non-branches of the facial artery: the infraorbital and lingual arteries

## canonical_key
anatomy.facial-artery.branches-and-non-branches

## definition
The facial artery gives off the inferior and superior labial, lateral nasal and angular branches along its course across the face. The infraorbital artery is instead a branch of the MAXILLARY artery, emerging through the infraorbital foramen, and the lingual artery is a separate, direct branch of the external carotid artery supplying the tongue — neither is a branch of the facial artery, despite both arising from vessels in the same external carotid territory.

## explicit_objective
Identify the infraorbital and lingual arteries as branches of the maxillary artery and the external carotid artery respectively, not of the facial artery.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-94153A6816FFE9

## label
The external jugular vein's dangerous point, 1½ inches above the clavicle

## canonical_key
anatomy.external-jugular-vein.dangerous-area

## definition
The external jugular vein is at its most dangerous about 1½ inches (roughly 4 cm) above the clavicle, where it pierces the deep (investing) cervical fascia to drain into the subclavian vein. Because the fascia holds the vein's wall open at this fixed point, a penetrating injury here cannot let the vein collapse, so it can entrain air into the negative-pressure thoracic venous system, risking a fatal air embolism.

## explicit_objective
State that the external jugular vein's dangerous point is roughly 1½ inches above the clavicle, where it pierces the deep cervical fascia, risking air embolism.

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the external jugular vein's own fascia-piercing danger point with the scalp's loose areolar (fourth) layer danger of avulsion — both are called 'dangerous' in different anatomical contexts for different reasons.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-35A269034066EB

## label
The great auricular nerve (C2-C3) supplies skin over the angle of the mandible

## canonical_key
anatomy.great-auricular-nerve.root-value-cutaneous-territory

## definition
The great auricular nerve, the largest ascending branch of the cervical plexus (root value C2-C3), curves around the posterior border of sternocleidomastoid to supply the skin over the parotid gland, the angle of the mandible and the lower auricle. Its territory is tested clinically by sensation over the angle of the mandible, distinct from the trigeminal nerves (zygomaticotemporal, zygomaticofacial) and the facial nerve, which is purely motor and carries essentially no cutaneous territory of its own.

## explicit_objective
State that the great auricular nerve (C2-C3) supplies skin over the angle of the mandible, and is examined by testing sensation there.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Great auricular nerve: root value and cutaneous territory

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Great auricular nerve|Root value C2 C3|Angle of mandible sensory nerve

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Attributing skin over the angle of the mandible to the facial nerve (which is motor, not sensory) or to a trigeminal branch (zygomaticotemporal/zygomaticofacial, whose territory lies elsewhere) instead of the great auricular nerve.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-BA080161837F03

## label
Buccal branch: mandibular nerve's is sensory, facial nerve's is motor

## canonical_key
anatomy.buccal-branch.mandibular-sensory-facial-motor

## definition
Two different nerves each carry a 'buccal branch': the mandibular (V3, trigeminal) buccal nerve is purely SENSORY, supplying the skin and mucosa of the cheek (buccal region), while the facial nerve's buccal branch is purely MOTOR, innervating buccinator and the upper-lip muscles of facial expression. The pairing mirrors the trigeminal (sensory face) vs. facial (motor face) division running through the whole cranial-nerve grid.

## explicit_objective
State that the mandibular nerve's buccal branch is sensory to the cheek while the facial nerve's buccal branch is motor to the facial muscles.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## primary_node_id

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Buccal branch mandibular nerve|Buccal branch facial nerve|Sensory vs motor buccal branch

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Reversing the two 'buccal branch' nerves' functions — it is the mandibular (trigeminal) buccal branch that is sensory and the facial nerve's buccal branch that is motor, the opposite pairing is a common error.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-E30CEA00BB4C3F

## label
An accessory nerve injury within the posterior triangle paralyses trapezius alone

## canonical_key
anatomy.accessory-nerve.posterior-triangle-trapezius

## definition
The spinal accessory nerve (CN XI) enters sternocleidomastoid, gives it a branch, then crosses the posterior triangle superficially (a site vulnerable during lymph node biopsy) before entering and supplying trapezius. An injury AT the posterior triangle is distal to the branch already given to sternocleidomastoid, so only trapezius is paralysed, producing a winged or drooped shoulder without neck weakness.

## explicit_objective
State that an accessory nerve injury within the posterior triangle paralyses trapezius alone, since the nerve has already supplied sternocleidomastoid by that point.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Accessory nerve injury site and its effect

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Accessory nerve posterior triangle injury|Trapezius paralysis|Spinal accessory nerve course

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming any accessory nerve injury paralyses both sternocleidomastoid and trapezius — the SITE of injury matters: sternocleidomastoid is spared once the nerve has already given it a branch before reaching the posterior triangle.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-BBDC83EF719120

## label
The muscles of facial expression are second pharyngeal arch derivatives

## canonical_key
anatomy.pharyngeal-arch.second-arch-facial-expression-muscles

## definition
The muscles of facial expression, together with stapedius, stylohyoid and the posterior belly of digastric, are all second (hyoid) pharyngeal arch derivatives, sharing that arch's own nerve, the facial nerve (CN VII) — which is exactly why a facial nerve lesion paralyses facial expression as a single functional and embryological unit. The first arch instead gives the muscles of mastication (trigeminal, V3); the third gives stylopharyngeus (glossopharyngeal); the fourth/sixth give the pharyngeal constrictors and laryngeal muscles (vagus).

## explicit_objective
State that the muscles of facial expression are second pharyngeal arch derivatives, supplied by the facial nerve.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Second arch: facial expression muscles

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Second pharyngeal arch muscles|Facial expression muscle embryology|Hyoid arch derivatives

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assigning the muscles of facial expression to the first arch (trigeminal) by analogy with the muscles of mastication — facial expression muscles are a second-arch, facial-nerve group, a separate arch and nerve from mastication.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-78EF99889CE564

## label
Facial nerve injury causes drooling and food pocketing in the cheek vestibule

## canonical_key
anatomy.facial-nerve.injury-food-pocketing-vestibule

## definition
Buccinator (a facial-nerve-supplied muscle of facial expression) normally keeps food compressed between the teeth by pressing the cheek inward. A facial nerve (CN VII) lesion paralyses buccinator and orbicularis oris, so food accumulates in the vestibule of the mouth (between cheek and gum) and saliva drools from the weak, incompetent lips and cheek.

## explicit_objective
Identify the facial nerve as the one injured when involuntary drooling and food-pocketing in the cheek vestibule occur together.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Facial nerve palsy drooling|Food pocketing cheek|Buccinator paralysis

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Attributing drooling and food pocketing to a trigeminal or vagus nerve lesion — the muscle at fault (buccinator, a muscle of facial expression) is facial-nerve-supplied, so the causative lesion is facial, not trigeminal or vagal.

## id
CON-NEU-BEC73868FF964D

## label
Omohyoid is innervated by the ansa cervicalis

## canonical_key
anatomy.infrahyoid-muscle.omohyoid-ansa-cervicalis

## definition
Omohyoid, one of the infrahyoid ('strap') muscles alongside sternohyoid and sternothyroid, is supplied by the ansa cervicalis, a loop formed from C1-C3 fibers of the cervical plexus. Thyrohyoid, though also an infrahyoid muscle, instead receives direct C1 fibers riding on the hypoglossal nerve, bypassing the ansa.

## explicit_objective
State that omohyoid, like sternohyoid and sternothyroid, is innervated by the ansa cervicalis.

anatomy.posterior-triangle.dissection-except-prevertebral-fascia

## definition
Dissecting a mass within the posterior triangle of the neck proceeds through skin, platysma, and the investing layer of deep cervical fascia (which splits to enclose sternocleidomastoid and trapezius and forms the roof of the triangle). The prevertebral fascia lies DEEP to the floor of the triangle (behind scalenus medius, levator scapulae, splenius capitis) — reaching it means the dissection has gone past the triangle's own contents entirely.

## explicit_objective
Identify the prevertebral fascia as the layer NOT needed to dissect down to a mass confined within the posterior triangle.

definition

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing the investing layer of deep fascia (the roof of the posterior triangle, opened early in dissection) with the prevertebral fascia (behind the triangle's floor, not needed for a mass confined to the triangle).

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-AA3EC009F3BC6E

## label
Buccinator and orbicularis oris: cheek-clearing and pronunciation

## canonical_key
anatomy.buccinator.pronunciation-and-food-clearance

## definition
Orbicularis oris, the sphincter muscle of the lips, is essential for pronunciation: it shapes and closes the oral aperture to form labial and labiodental consonant sounds. Buccinator, the 'trumpeter's muscle' of the cheek, has a separate but related job: it contracts to keep the cheek flat against the teeth during chewing, pushing food back onto the occlusal surfaces and preventing it from pooling in the vestibule between cheek and gum; buccinator weakness (e.g. in facial nerve palsy) lets food accumulate there.

## explicit_objective
State that orbicularis oris is essential for pronunciation while buccinator keeps food from collecting in the vestibule of the mouth.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic
Buccinator and orbicularis oris function

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Orbicularis oris pronunciation|Buccinator function|Vestibule of mouth food clearance

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing buccinator (cheek muscle, clears food from the vestibule) with orbicularis oris (lip sphincter, essential for pronunciation) — the two muscles of facial expression have distinct, non-interchangeable roles in this region.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-24A31F97048FBC

## label
The infraorbital nerve is a maxillary, not ophthalmic, branch

## canonical_key
anatomy.ophthalmic-nerve.branches-and-non-branches

## definition
The ophthalmic (V1) division of the trigeminal nerve gives the supraorbital, supratrochlear, infratrochlear, lacrimal and nasociliary branches. The infraorbital nerve is instead a branch of the MAXILLARY nerve (V2), emerging through the infraorbital foramen to supply the lower eyelid, cheek and upper lip — a different division from the ophthalmic nerve's own branches.

## explicit_objective
Identify the infraorbital nerve, a maxillary (V2) branch, as the one option that is NOT a branch of the ophthalmic nerve, while supraorbital, infratrochlear and lacrimal genuinely are.

## concept_type
definition

## status

## support_mode

## subject

## primary_node_id

## secondary_node_ids

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Ophthalmic nerve branches|Infraorbital nerve division|Trigeminal V1 vs V2

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming any nerve near the orbit (infraorbital) must be an ophthalmic (V1) branch because of the name's proximity to the eye — the infraorbital nerve is a maxillary (V2) branch instead.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-02BEAF5AB68C54

## label
The retromandibular vein's anterior division joins the facial vein, posterior joins to form the external jugular

## canonical_key
anatomy.retromandibular-vein.branches-to-facial-external-jugular

## definition
The retromandibular vein, formed within the parotid gland by the union of the superficial temporal and maxillary veins, splits into an anterior division that joins the facial vein (forming the common facial vein, draining to the internal jugular vein) and a posterior division that joins the posterior auricular vein to form the external jugular vein — two separate downstream pathways from the same parent vessel.

## explicit_objective
State that the retromandibular vein's anterior division unites with the facial vein while its posterior division unites to form the external jugular vein.

definition

## status

## support_mode

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Retromandibular vein divisions|Common facial vein|External jugular vein formation

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Reversing the retromandibular vein's two divisions — it is the ANTERIOR division that joins the facial vein and the POSTERIOR division that forms the external jugular vein, not the other way round.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-C299342502BEE2

## label
Digastric's intermediate tendon perforates stylohyoid

## canonical_key
anatomy.digastric.intermediate-tendon-perforates-stylohyoid

## definition
Digastric's intermediate tendon, connecting its anterior and posterior bellies, is held down near the hyoid bone by a fibrous sling and characteristically perforates (pierces through) stylohyoid as it passes — a distinctive anatomical relationship used to identify the tendon surgically, distinct from digastric's separate, better-known dual-nerve-supply fact (anterior belly via V3, posterior belly via CN VII).

## explicit_objective

## label
The floor of the posterior triangle is formed by splenius capitis, levator scapulae and scalenus medius

## definition
The floor of the posterior triangle of the neck is formed by (from above down) splenius capitis, levator scapulae, and scalenus medius and posterior. Together these muscles form the muscular floor over which the triangle's contents (accessory nerve, cervical plexus branches, part of the brachial plexus) run.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-F2AA9E14F64076

## label
The inferior belly of omohyoid is the muscle content of the posterior triangle

## canonical_key
anatomy.posterior-triangle.content-muscle-omohyoid

## definition
The inferior belly of omohyoid crosses the lower part of the posterior triangle before passing deep to sternocleidomastoid and becoming the superior belly — making it the one part of omohyoid genuinely counted among the posterior triangle's contents, unlike its own superior belly (anterior triangle) or the digastric bellies (suprahyoid region, not the posterior triangle).

## explicit_objective
Identify the inferior belly of omohyoid as the muscle found within the posterior triangle.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Posterior triangle of the neck

## subtopic
Muscle contents of the posterior triangle

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Posterior triangle contents|Inferior belly of omohyoid|Omohyoid two bellies

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assuming the whole omohyoid muscle, or its SUPERIOR belly, lies within the posterior triangle — only the INFERIOR belly crosses the posterior triangle; the superior belly lies in the anterior triangle.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-94E3325FD7E50D

## label
The vertebral artery is found within the suboccipital triangle

## canonical_key
anatomy.vertebral-artery.suboccipital-triangle

## definition
After ascending through the foramina transversaria of the upper six cervical vertebrae and winding behind the lateral mass of the atlas, the vertebral artery passes through the suboccipital triangle (bounded by rectus capitis posterior major and the two oblique muscles) before piercing the posterior atlanto-occipital membrane and dura to enter the skull.

## explicit_objective
State that the vertebral artery, after leaving the foramen transversarium of the atlas, is found within the suboccipital triangle.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic

## subtopic

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Vertebral artery course|Suboccipital triangle|Foramen transversarium of atlas

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Placing the vertebral artery within the posterior triangle of the neck proper — its course through the neck lies in the suboccipital triangle, a distinct, deeper region formed by different muscles.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-0BC37627246BB5

## label
The sympathetic chain lies outside the carotid sheath

## canonical_key
anatomy.carotid-sheath.contents-except-sympathetic-chain

## definition
The carotid sheath contains the common and internal carotid arteries, the internal jugular vein and the vagus nerve. The (cervical) sympathetic chain lies POSTERIOR to the carotid sheath, embedded in the prevertebral fascia against the vertebral column's transverse processes, not actually enclosed within the sheath itself.

## explicit_objective
Identify the sympathetic chain as the structure that lies outside, not within, the carotid sheath.

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Including the sympathetic chain among the carotid sheath's contents because of its proximity — the chain runs posterior to the sheath, embedded in the prevertebral fascia, not within the sheath's own contents.

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-16964870332712

## label
Emissary veins directly connect scalp veins to the dural venous sinuses

## canonical_key
anatomy.emissary-vein.scalp-to-dural-sinus-connection

## definition
Emissary veins are valveless vessels that pass through skull foramina (e.g. parietal foramen, foramen ovale, mastoid foramen) to directly connect the extracranial scalp venous network with the intracranial dural venous sinuses — the anatomical route by which a scalp infection can spread inward to cause sinus thrombosis, distinct from cerebral veins (draining brain substance) and diploic veins (running within the diploe of the skull bone).

## explicit_objective
State that emissary veins directly connect scalp veins to the dural venous sinuses.

## concept_type
definition

## status
under review

## support_mode
direct_statement

## subject
neuro

## primary_node_id
DIS-ANA-T07

## secondary_node_ids

## topic
Scalp and dural venous drainage

## subtopic
Emissary veins: scalp-to-sinus connection

## microtopic

## nanotopic

## modules

## aliases
Emissary vein function|Scalp vein to dural sinus|Valveless emissary vein

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Confusing emissary veins (scalp-to-sinus, through skull foramina) with diploic veins (within the diploe of the skull bone itself) — both connect to the dural sinuses but along different routes.

## label
The pretracheal nodes belong to the deep, not superficial, cervical lymphatic group

## canonical_key
anatomy.lymph-node.transverse-superficial-except-pretracheal

## definition
The superficial (transverse) pericervical collar of lymph nodes includes the submental, submandibular, preauricular (parotid), mastoid and occipital nodes. The pretracheal nodes instead belong to the deep (vertical) cervical lymph node group, draining the trachea, thyroid and larynx.

## arabic_aliases

# Item

## id
CON-NEU-55AC808921C89F

## label
The retropharyngeal nodes belong to the deep (vertical) cervical lymphatic group

## canonical_key
anatomy.lymph-node.vertical-deep-retropharyngeal

## definition
The retropharyngeal nodes are part of the deep (vertical) cervical lymphatic group, lying behind the pharynx and draining the nasopharynx, paranasal sinuses and middle ear before onward drainage into the deep cervical chain along the internal jugular vein — distinct from the superficial pericervical collar (occipital, preauricular, submental, submandibular nodes).

## explicit_objective
Identify the retropharyngeal nodes as belonging to the deep (vertical) cervical lymphatic group.

Retropharyngeal lymph node|Deep cervical lymphatic group|Vertical lymph node chain

## arabic_label

## arabic_aliases

## pitfalls
Assigning the retropharyngeal nodes to the superficial pericervical collar because of head/neck proximity — they belong to the deep, vertical lymphatic chain draining the nasopharynx and middle ear.
